0,2026-09-17,"Citrini Research says that AI-enabled robotics are a budding mega-trend for investors to pay attention to. 
The firm, which went viral for a hypothetical AI doomsday scenario, laid out its bullish thesis on robotics as the next growth phase of the AI trade, noting the progress being made in China. It highlighted the World Humanoid Robot Games, held in Beijing in August, as a significant forward leap for the technology. 
""Humanoid Olympics may seem like an exercise in the absurd, but really are a top-down strategic effort to push the limits of hardware in a competitive, visible and verifiable setting,"" Citrini wrote. ""Sending a humanoid at full sprint into a crash wall is literally 'moving fast and breaking things.' Scoff at your own risk.""
""Physical AI"" is in fact already getting attention in tech and investing circles. Uber founder Travis Kalanick's physical automation startup Atom is expanding rapidly. Meanwhile, Wall Street forecasters such as Ulrike Hoffmann-Burchardi of UBS are touting the benefits of robotics stocks as a key component of the AI trade.
But Citrini made it clear that it doesn't think US investors are appreciating the growth potential that robotics offer — or the threat posed by China as it advances toward cornering the market. While the firm acknowledged that the US may be ahead when it comes to software and decision making systems, China has an edge. 
The report highlighted strong similarities between today's robotics market and the booming EV industry of a few years ago.
""It's running the EV playbook, with the literal same players,"" they stated. ""Tesla popularized the modern EV and Chinese companies like XPeng replicated.""
Indeed, China's race to beat the US proved a major development that furthered the global EV boom, as Chinese automakers such as BYD have outsold Tesla and undercut US companies on cost. In June 2026, its CEO revealed plans to start incorporating robots in every showroom that would help sell cars.
Meanwhile, XPeng has gone all in on robotics as well, beginning work on XPeng Iron, its own humanoid robot, intended to rival Tesla's Optimus. Both XPeng and Tesla have struggled throughout the year, but Citrini likes the Chinese EV stock as a bet on a looming robotics boom.

1,2026-09-17,"David Einhorn says younger generations' own habits are holding them back from breaking into the housing market or building wealth. 
The billionaire founder of Greenlight Capital recently speculated about the reasons younger Americans are falling behind in homeownership. The main reason, in Einhorn's view, is that many young people lack the patience and investment discipline needed to save up for a home, and are funneling their wealth into speculative assets or sports betting. 
""I sense the younger generation is just more impatient. They'd rather, you know, speculate in crypto or speculate in stocks or speculate on sporting events and try to build wealth by guessing those things correctly,"" Einhorn said, speaking in a recent episode of Morgan Stanley's ""Break the Playbook"" series.
""And some of them will have some success with that, and others will have less success,"" he added.
Einhorn said younger people are exhibiting a shift in ""philosophy,"" with many wondering why they should front the extra cash to build equity in a home when renting can be cheaper than owning a mortgage.
But owning a home still makes sense from a long-term financial perspective, he said, pointing to the potential for home equity appreciation and how a house often serves as a ""savings vehicle.""
""Buying a house and paying off your mortgage over 30 years, you know, that requires long-term patience and discipline, and that might be in short supply,"" Einhorn said.
Younger Americans are less likely to own a home, a trend that has emerged amid a broader slowdown in the housing market driven by high mortgage rates and near-record home prices.
The average 30-year fixed mortgage rate rose to 6.76% last week, up from

The job market, another factor that influences homeownership rates, has also been daunting for younger entry-level workers.
In 2025, 38% of Gen Zers aged 28 owned a home, compared with 42% of Gen Xers and 44% of boomers at that age, according to a Redfin analysis.
Meanwhile, 57% of millennials aged 36 owned a home last year, compared to 61% of Gen Xers and 63% of boomers at that age.
Younger Americans feeling locked out of the housing market is also a major force driving the boom in retail investing, a 2025 report from JPMorganChase Institute found. Last year, 37% of 25-year-olds added funds to a retail investment account, up sixfold from levels a decade ago, the bank said in a report.
Home prices have appreciated more slowly than red-hot stock prices in recent years, another factor that may be pushing younger Americans toward investing rather than saving for a home. The S&P Case-Shiller US National Home Price Index has appreciated by 85% over the last decade, compared to the 253% increase in the S&P 500 in that time.

As oil prices continue to rise, Costco puts the brakes on how much shoppers can stock up on.
Copied to clipboard
Costco is known for letting members bulk buy just about anything. Motor oil just became the exception.
The retailer has capped purchases of its Kirkland Signature full synthetic motor oil to two units every seven days, according to CNBC. The price for a two-pack of 5-quart bottles has also jumped from around $30 to $57.99. Mobil 1 is facing similar limits, capped at five units per membership at $43.99 for a 6-pack.
The squeeze traces back to oil markets. Brent crude, the leading global benchmark for pricing internationally traded crude oil, climbed above $109 a barrel this week, its highest level since May. As crude prices climb, refiners have been prioritizing gasoline and diesel production over base oil, the key ingredient in synthetic motor oil, further squeezing supply.
The ripple effects are showing up everywhere. Gasoline hit a Labor Day record this year, and U.S. diesel prices have climbed above $6 a gallon for the first time. Even a warehouse club known for bulk deals and bargain prices isn’t immune.

3,2026-09-17,"You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?
Here’s what I learned from a failed international expansion — and how founders can validate a market before committing capital.
Copied to clipboard

		Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.	
What’s actually holding you back from entering new markets? I bet you think the real barrier is wasting capital or choosing the wrong geography. But what if I told you the real risk starts much earlier, long before any of those decisions are made?
I’ve spent 20 years launching more than 100 companies across 25 jurisdictions, building complex global structures and running cross‑border licensing. And despite all that experience, my toughest lessons came from my own missteps.
When I expanded one of my projects into China, I made the classic mistake. I assumed that a model that excelled at home would automatically succeed abroad. We entered the market with the same product, positioning and even website design, convinced that a local partner would fill in the gaps.
It failed because I treated expansion as replication, not reinvention.
I keep watching founders repeat the same error. They start with incorporation documents, as if international expansion were a legal procedure. It isn’t. It’s a strategic shift that demands validation, adaptation and discipline.
“No market need” is still the number‑one reason companies fail, responsible for 42% of post‑mortems, according to CB Insights. In fact, entering a foreign market without context or demand signals is equivalent to operating blind in an unfamiliar terrain with no map and no understanding of the local rules.
The good news is that global scaling becomes straightforward once you approach it correctly. My own approach now rests on a three‑step framework that lets you validate demand before you spend a single dollar on incorporation and ensures you enter new markets with intention, not just hope.
The first question in any expansion is never “How do we register here?” It’s “Is there real, local demand for what we offer in the form we offer it?”
When we expanded into China, we fell into the classic copy‑paste trap. We assumed our proven model would travel unchanged. We didn’t pressure‑test whether the market wanted our specific value proposition, nor did we adjust to local operating norms. That oversight cost time, capital and momentum.
It may seem strange, but success at home can become a hindrance abroad. The instincts you have honed, the customer behavior patterns you rely on and the pricing logic that feels like second nature — all of these can work against you the moment you cross the border.
In our current licensing and corporate services business, we scaled across Europe and major fintech hubs precisely because we rebuilt our service mix around actual local demand.
Your actionable checklist:
Laws matter, but local market mechanics matter more. Opening an entity or hiring contractors means nothing if you don’t understand how trust is built and how business is actually conducted in that culture. Without that insight, every operational decision becomes guesswork.
From my practice, founders often routinely miscalculate the true cost of operating in a new jurisdiction. They budget for registration fees and basic legal work but overlook the structural expenses that define long‑term viability, such as local director mandates, physical‑presence requirements, compliance audits, banking friction, payroll taxes, statutory filings and mandatory legal retainers. 
Some jurisdictions allow you to operate efficiently with a lightweight license. Others impose statutory obligations that can completely reshape your unit economics.
Your actionable checklist:
What helps me is thinking of international expansion the same way you’d approach relocating your family to a new country. As a rule, you don’t buy a house, enroll your kids in school or sign long‑term contracts before you’ve walked the neighborhood, understood the climate and secured income.
Business expansion demands the same discipline because premature commitments lock you into costs you may never recover.
Only once you’ve confirmed real demand, true operating costs and cultural fit should you authorize execution. Anything earlier is speculation disguised as strategy.
Your actionable checklist:
I’d go as far as to say that expansions collapse in year one because they were built on assumptions that never matched reality.
If you test demand first, model your real economics, understand cultural mechanics and set strict entry KPIs, you eliminate 80% of the costliest mistakes founders make abroad.
What you end up with is a framework you can rely on again and again — validate demand, validate economics, validate cultural fit, and only then move into execution. That sequence has saved me more times than I can count, and it’s the backbone of every successful expansion I’ve led since.
What’s actually holding you back from entering new markets? I bet you think the real barrier is wasting capital or choosing the wrong geography. But what if I told you the real risk starts much earlier, long before any of those decisions are made?

4,2026-09-17,"Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney has welcomed the European Union's ambition to make his country the first associate member of the bloc â but what would that mean in practice?
Carney says he is seeking a ""unique"" relationship with the EU, in an apparent reference to the fact that EU treaties do not currently provide for associate membership.
Germany had proposed Ukrainian associate membership earlier this year but Kyiv rejected the idea, saying it only wanted full EU integration. The EU itself also seemed reluctant.
But EU chief Ursula von der Leyen has now shifted the focus across the Atlantic to Canada
At the her State of the Union address on Wednesday, von der Leyen listed several key areas for cooperation, although she did not give specific details about how Canadian associate membership would work. 
Carney told reporters on Thursday that Canada was ""not in a position, nor seeking, to become a full member of the European Union"".
He also said it was not an attempt to create a ""third bloc"", adding: ""We do not seek power to dominate others.""
He told the European Parliament on Thursday that closer alignment between Ottawa and the EU was needed to confront the ""current geopolitical rupture"" â nodding to strained relations with the US following President Donald Trump's tariff policy and the collapse of trade talks between the two countries.
At the European Parliament on Thursday, Carney set out what he views as key areas where his country and the EU can cooperate, including:
AI
critical minerals
defence industrial capacity
energy security
the space sector
Carney also said he wanted to move towards membership of the EU's Erasmus Plus scheme â  the funding programme which allows students to work and study abroad. All 27 EU states are members and there are seven non-EU members. The UK will rejoin the scheme in 2027.
He said Canada could contribute liquefied natural gas (LNG) and hydrogen ""at large scale to support Europe's energy security"" and that Canada in turn could benefit from what he described as Europe's leadership on clean energy technologies.
So we know what it is not â full membership of the EU.
It is not clear is what exactly the relationship would look like. EU associate membership does not currently exist and the process to create it could take years.
If associate status were enshrined in EU treaties, it would require unanimity and ratification among all 27 member states.
Despite von der Leyen's overtures towards Canada, some EU countries would prefer to build on existing defence and trade agreements rather than create a new form of membership.
The EU is Canada's second-largest trading partner and the far-reaching Comprehensive Economic and Trade Agreement was agreed more than a decade ago, though several of the bloc's 27 members are yet to ratify that agreement.
The current trade deal does not remove border checks. Would associate membership change that?
It also remains to be seen what associate membership would mean for Canada's relationship with the European single market.
Were Canada to one day join the single market, it would have to match EU regulations and standards while having no say on policy â in a similar way to European Economic Area countries Norway, Iceland and Liechtenstein, or Switzerland, which has a separate deal.
Canada's rules have historically been geared towards its biggest trade partner, the US.
And then there's the matter of other countries seeking to join the bloc.
Albania, Bosnia and Herzegovina, Montenegro, North Macedonia and Serbia are already on the long road towards full EU membership, while in Eastern Europe, Georgia and Moldova are also pursuing membership.
There is no suggestion that a new relationship with Canada would hinder these countries' progress towards full accession, but any impression that Ottawa was being given special treatment could create tension.
There is also the question of Trump's response. He has described the prospect of Canada's associate membership as ""laughable"" and has threatened further tariffs on the EU if the plan goes ahead.

If you can provide the cheapest, fastest and best option, there will be a line out the door for your service or product.
Most providers know, though, that good, fast and inexpensive are trade-offs. You will have trouble offering all three.
And in a competitive marketplace, as soon as someone starts racing to the bottom and cutting corners, keeping up is brutal.
The alternative is to walk away from the race.
Instead, offer just one: You’ll pay a lot but you’ll get more than you pay for.
In every market I can think of, there’s always demand for an option that is noticeably faster.
And even more so, there’s a market for something that’s obviously better.
The hard part isn’t finding the market. The hard part is keeping the promise. 
If you were required to charge five times what you charge now, how would you change what you offer?

6,2026-09-17,"The first rate hike in three years is in the books. The Fed is officially taking direct steps to slow inflation.
Meanwhile, the equity market's worst-performing sector this year has already been pricing in a slowdown. Anyone caught off-guard by the recent consensus around a rate hike clearly hasn't been paying attention to consumer discretionary stocks.
Prior to the Fed decision, the S&P 500 Consumer Discretionary sector was down 5% year to date, badly lagging the benchmark's 11% gain. And according to historical research done by Goldman Sachs, the group is due for more pain, based on past reactions to higher rates. It fell another 0.7% on Wednesday.
It makes sense when you consider the current backdrop. Higher mortgage rates and increased auto-loan and credit-card costs can leave households with less of a cushion for non-urgent purchases.
But the group can't be painted with a broad brush. Its weakness hasn't been universal: Best Buy and Garmin are each up more than 35% year to date, with eBay and Ross Stores right behind. Different companies represent different economic dynamics that all feed into the performance of the unloved consumer discretionary sector.
Let's break the group's low-performing companies into three categories, with each reflecting a different way higher rates can pressure discretionary spending:
YTD stock return: -20%
Lowe's offers the most direct link between higher rates and consumer spending. Large home-improvement projects often depend on a homeowner's willingness to borrow money or tap home equity. Higher borrowing costs can make those decisions easier to put off.
This isn't to say that the home-improvement space is down for the count. Repairs still need to be made to existing homes, and Lowe's has experience weathering past housing slowdowns.

But in the age of AI, a stable, rate-sensitive business may not offer the kind of growth story that investors are hungry for.
YTD stock return: -54% for LULU, -44% for NKE
This is a different type of consumer calculation. Shoppers buying from these two companies don't need loans, but they're still paying extra for products that may be trendy and high-quality — yet they can ultimately live without.
Retailers like this can feel the pinch early when consumers start to pull back. They might still spend, but they're more likely to trade down to a lower-cost competitor.
Of course, both companies have firm-specific issues of their own. Both are in the middle of turnaround projects following prolonged periods of underperformance. Their share prices have been under pressure as investors weigh rising competition and cloudy growth outlooks.
YTD stock return: -19%
Compared to the companies above, McDonald's is in a unique position. It can be a final destination for bargain-seekers. In theory, it should benefit as diners shift away from more expensive restaurant options.
But its performance still offers valuable consumer insight. If traffic begins to slow, that may signal that consumers are cutting back on spending entirely, rather than simply reallocating it in a more cost-efficient way. If there's noticeable weakness in smaller, everyday purchases, then it might really be time to worry.
Of course, consumer discretionary doesn't speak for the economy as a whole. Growth in other areas can offset one industry's struggles. But it can nonetheless offer some helpful hints about the direction of the US consumer.

7,2026-09-17,"Rising fears of an AI apocalypse have rattled markets and forced investors to reckon with a potentially existential risk.
A future where robots take over and terminate humans remains a radical vision, fueled by recent warnings from AI researchers who consider doomsday a possible, but very unlikely, scenario.
Yet it's worth asking how people can protect their wealth from AI Armageddon. We reached out to several investing and personal finance gurus for guidance, and here's what they said:
Wilson said he's confident that if a catastrophe comes, then ""gold is going to be the one asset class that will endure.""
Cybersecurity stocks could be the ""best defense"" in the meantime, he said, as they stand to benefit from the events that would likely precede an AI apocalypse, such as Anthropic's recent warning that AI developers should slow down to allow risk prevention to catch up.
""So even if we don't get the meltdown, we get enough worry and enough of a reaction to boost defenses,"" Wilson said.
If there is a market meltdown, he continued, the best bets might be ""miners, real estate, gold, plus things like defense and of course booze and cigarettes as we'll all need something to keep us from going insane!""
Longo championed core investing principles such as having a diversified portfolio both across and within asset classes to avoid dangerous concentration, setting aside some cash, and limiting leverage.
The Rutgers professor said that for mitigating AI doomsday risks, ""traditional safe-haven assets"" such as gold, US Treasurys, and Swiss francs might be worth a look.

Longo added that within equities, ""software security stocks and defense stocks may attract investor attention.""
Worried investors shouldn't ""scramble for a single financial life raft"" but instead limit their exposure to an AI disaster, Streeter said. That could mean paring their bets on the biggest US tech stocks, maintaining a ""sensible cash buffer,"" and ""spreading investments across regions, sectors and asset classes.""
She added that gold can be a ""useful hedge in periods of severe market stress,"" but crypto is unlikely to ""provide much shelter from an AI-induced storm"" given its historical volatility when investors are ""running for cover.""
Streeter cautioned against ""trying to jump overboard altogether,"" as investors could miss out on gains if everything turns out OK. She said that ""diversification and keeping sight of the longer horizon may prove more valuable than trying to time a hasty exit.""
If disaster strikes, government bonds, gold, real estate funds, and high-quality, defensive stocks are likely to be investors' best bets, Ozkardeskaya said.
For stock investors, ""rotating to companies with strong earnings, low leverage, and sufficient free cash flow would be a no-brainer,"" she said, adding that ""defensive names would become sexy.""
Coles warned against overstating the threat of AI ending the world, as it remains ""completely theoretical"" and ""safeguards"" will likely be put in place to stop it.
But she said those worried could stockpile long-lasting food and household essentials such as toilet paper, in case there's a ""catastrophic global collapse"" or they can't leave their homes for health reasons.
She also said people might ""keep a small sum of money somewhere safe at home, in case there are any interruptions to their access to cash."" But she advised against stashing away a large amount of cash as it could be stolen or damaged.
""It's important not to panic at news like this and make major changes that could damage your finances if life continues as normal,"" she said. ""However, there's nothing to be lost in taking a handful of smaller steps.""
""If we're truly talking about a catastrophic AI scenario, there are no borders, and there are no languages, and money may not mean very much,"" Pugh said.
She said that debating between cash, gold, or stocks doesn't seem ""particularly useful"" in that scenario, as all of those assets depend on ""functioning human systems.""
""This would be a species-level problem,"" Pugh said. ""So I would focus on the asset that matters most in that scenario: our relationships with other humans — our families, our neighbors, and our communities.""
She said that her goal would be gathering her loved ones and reminding each other why humanity is worth preserving: ""our connection to one another and our freedom to choose how we live and how we connect.""
Pugh added that ""on a more practical level, maybe keep some water and food around, too.""

8,2026-09-17,"I am currently in the job market, so I receive some recruiter emails. Recently, I started seeing a slightly different kind of message.
I posted in Hacker News' ""Who wants to be hired?"" thread with a short description of my background: physics, C++, CUDA, GPU programming, machine learning, and some recent work around LLM inference.
A little while later, several different people contacted me about the same Senior Software Engineer position. Their messages mentioned specific parts of my background, but they all led to the same company. Each included a different referral code.
The job ad says a successful referral pays $1,500. The payment only happens if the candidate is hired through that referral, so this is not a complaint about people being paid for every application.
The problem is what that incentive does when the referral program is public.
I am not an obvious fit for the role. I am finishing a PhD and moving toward industry, while the position is explicitly senior. I have some relevant technical overlap, but I would not describe myself as the established senior engineer the title suggests.
It seems unlikely that several unrelated people independently studied my experience and all decided I was an unusually strong candidate for the same senior position. A more likely explanation is that my comment, along with the other posts in the thread, was searched for keywords. I looked vaguely plausible, and sending me a message was cheap compared with the chance of earning $1,500.
The messages are also easy to personalize at scale. An LLM can produce a note that mentions my background in physics, CUDA, or LLM inference without the sender knowing much more about me than those keywords.
I do not particularly blame the people who contacted me. They are responding rationally, from their perspective, to the incentives the company created. But from the candidate's perspective, the result is spam: several unsolicited messages from strangers, each trying to get credit if I eventually get hired.
A traditional referral usually carries some information. Someone knows the candidate, has worked with them, has seen their work, or at least knows enough about them to say, ""I think this person is worth talking to.""
Here, the referrer may know nothing about me beyond a public comment and a few matching keywords. The referral code mainly determines who gets the bonus if I am hired. It does not explain why that person is in a position to recommend me.
That is not necessarily dishonest. It is simply closer to candidate sourcing than to a personal referral.
There is a cost for the company, too. A public referral program can generate more outreach and more applications without producing better candidates. Recruiters have more noise to filter, candidates receive more quasi-personalized messages, and the referral itself carries less information.
A company may reasonably decide that $1,500 is worth paying for a successful hire. The issue is not the size of the payment or the fact that it is conditional. The issue is making the payment available to anyone willing to send cold emails to public profiles.
Referrals work better when the referrer is expected to know something about the person being recommended. Broad candidate sourcing can be handled through recruiters, job boards, communities, and advertising.
Once a referral program becomes a public competition for attribution, candidates stop experiencing it as a recommendation. They experience it as spam.
So please don't make job referrals public."

I had a very interesting conversation with Michael Monaghan of Founder’s Fund about HNTI; here is the description:
Barry Ritholtz explains why getting rich through investing is simpler than most people think, why market forecasts are so often wrong, and why your own behavior may be the biggest threat to long-term investment success.
Michael Monaghan sits down with Barry Ritholtz, Chairman and Chief Investment Officer of Ritholtz Wealth Management, host of Masters in Business, and author of How Not to Invest, for a wide-ranging conversation on stock market investing, compounding, index funds, active management, investor psychology, financial media, market predictions, and building wealth over time.
Barry explains why investors should focus on getting rich slowly, why humans are psychologically wired to make poor decisions in volatile markets, and why the most confident market forecasts can often be the least reliable.
They discuss why so few active managers outperform over long periods, what Barry learned from Warren Buffett, Ray Dalio, Howard Marks, and Daniel Kahneman, and why he believes most investors should keep the core of their portfolio simple. Barry also shares his “cowboy account” strategy for investors who still want to pick stocks, including how he thinks about Nvidia and speculative investments without putting long-term wealth at risk.
The conversation also explores what financial advisors actually add beyond investment performance, direct indexing and tax-loss harvesting, and why Barry believes the purpose of money is not simply to accumulate a bigger number.

Tech > Mobile > Headphones
Bose's upgraded $299 top-of-the-line open buds feature improved sound and battery life, while the totally new $199 Sport Open Earbuds are designed for active use.
Executive Editor / Reviews
Executive Editor / Reviews
September 17, 2026, 1:15 pm ET
Bose’s original Ultra Open Earbuds were released more than two years ago and sparked the proliferation of a new style of open earbuds that resemble clip-on earrings. I’d been waiting for the Ultra Open Earbuds (2nd Gen) to arrive, and now they’re finally here, with improved sound, voice-calling performance and battery life, Bose says. 
While the updated buds carry the same elevated $299 list price as their predecessor, the company has also unveiled its completely new Sport Open Earbuds that cost $199 and have a different design than the original Ultra Open Earbuds. Both new buds are available for preorder Sept. 19 and ship Oct. 1.
Read more: Best Clip-On Earbuds of 2026
According to Bose, the Ultra Open Earbuds (2nd Gen) feature new drivers, allowing them to play louder and deliver deeper bass. Like Bose’s QuietComfort Ultra Earbuds (2nd Gen), as well as its QuietComfort Ultra Headphones (2nd Gen) and QuietComfort Headphones (2nd Gen), they’re also equipped with the company’s TrueSpatial technology with head-tracking for Immersive Audio, which now includes a Cinema mode. They look very similar to the original, with a slightly upgraded version of the original’s “cuff-clip form factor” that a lot of other people found quite comfortable.
Available in black, white smoke and “limited-edition” cherry chocolate or olive green colors, they have an IPX4 rating (splash-resistant) and are rated for up to 9 hours of battery life (that’s up from 7 hours), though that number drops if you engage Immersive Audio. 
Thanks to their secure fit and open design, the Ultra Open Earbuds worked well for running and biking, and the new version is also suitable for sporting activities, though Bose is billing the lighter Sport Open Earbuds as more durable and “purpose-built for active use,” with an even more secure fit.
The Sport Open Earbuds look similar to Shokz OpenDots 2 earbuds, which also sell for $199. Bose says they “combine a secure cuff-style fit with soft silicone construction, IP54 sweat, splash and dust resistance, tactile controls and rich open audio, all while keeping listeners naturally aware of their surroundings.”
The Sport Open Earbuds don’t support Bose’s Immersive Audio feature and are rated for up to 7 hours of battery life. They’re available in three colors: black, white smoke and “limited-edition” eucalyptus green (that same color is available for Bose’s recently released QuietComfort (2nd Gen) headphones).
I haven’t tried the new buds yet, but I’ll be receiving review samples in advance of their Oct. 1 ship date and will post full hands-on reviews of them.
A lot has changed in the clip-on earbuds market since Bose first released the Ultra Open Earbuds in February 2024, with dozens of new clip-on models available, many of them at far lower prices than $300. While I suspect the Ultra Open Earbuds (2nd Gen) will be among the best-sounding open earbuds and certainly a good premium option in the category, I also think we’ll see these buds on sale fairly frequently; $300 just seems too high to pay for a pair of clip-on earbuds. (Apple’s new AirPods 5 don’t fit as securely as either of these open buds, but they do feature active noise canceling and start at $129.)
As for the Sport Open Earbuds, I’ll see how they compare to the Shokz OpenDots 2, Anker Aero Clip 2 and Baseus’ XC1 clip-on earbuds ($100), which feature Sound by Bose. Like the Ultra Open Earbuds (2nd Gen), I also expect the Sport Open Buds to see regular discounts to compete with all the new premium sport clip-on buds hitting the market, many of which feature improved sound and voice-calling performance.

13,2026-09-17,A British Pokémon card store has denied giving celebrities special treatment during a 30th Anniversary Pokémon card launch that left customers who had queued for hours empty handed.
14,2026-09-17,"AI images are taking over stock libraries, but the human-made web isn’t dead yet. We found 25 places where designers can still find incredible free, real photography, art, and imagery for commercial use.
Posted September 17, 2026
Posted Sep 17, 2026
Finding a free image used to be simple. Search Unsplash, download something tasteful, and move on with your life. Now there’s another question: Was this thing actually photographed?
AI-generated imagery is rapidly filling stock libraries, search results, social feeds, and asset marketplaces. That’s not necessarily bad—AI can be an incredibly useful design tool—but sometimes you want an actual photograph taken by an actual human. Or an illustration made with paint. Or a botanical drawing created by somebody who spent six hours staring at a leaf rather than six seconds writing a prompt.
And as generated images become increasingly polished, the little imperfections of real photography are starting to feel strangely luxurious. The good news is that the human-made web hasn’t disappeared yet. Far from it.
Here are 25 places designers can still find free photography, artwork, illustrations, historical imagery, textures, and other visual assets that can be used in commercial projects. Some are entirely focused on human-made work; others require you to pay attention to the individual asset and its license.
Unsplash remains the obvious first stop, and there’s a reason it became practically synonymous with free stock photography.
Its enormous library covers everything from architecture and landscapes to portraits, technology, food, interiors, and abstract textures. More importantly for designers, much of the photography feels editorial rather than traditionally “stock.” You’ll find cinematic lighting, interesting compositions, negative space for typography, and plenty of images capable of carrying an entire hero section.
Images covered by the Unsplash License can generally be used for commercial projects without attribution, although crediting photographers is encouraged.
Best for: Hero images, lifestyle photography, architecture, travel, editorial layouts, backgrounds.
Why designers should care: The search is excellent and the sheer size of the library means you can usually find something usable within minutes.
And here’s something particularly useful in the age of AI: Unsplash currently says it does not accept generative-AI content. Its submission rules prohibit images created using generative AI, making it one of the more straightforward large libraries when you’re specifically looking for human-created photography.
Watch out: The Unsplash License doesn’t magically clear every right attached to what’s pictured. Recognizable people, trademarks, artwork, and some properties can still require additional permissions depending on how you use the photograph.
Pexels is probably Unsplash’s closest competitor—and depending on what you’re searching for, occasionally the better one.
It has a huge collection covering people, business, technology, interiors, cities, nature, fashion, and just about every other category imaginable. It also has an excellent collection of free stock video, making it especially valuable for designers working on animated hero sections, social content, presentations, or motion-heavy websites.
Pexels allows its photos and videos to be used commercially, attribution isn’t required, and assets can be modified.
Best for: Websites, campaigns, social media, presentations, video backgrounds.
Why designers should care: Having photography and video under one roof can dramatically speed up projects requiring a consistent visual direction across formats.
Better still for this list, Pexels explicitly prohibits contributors from uploading generative-AI photos and videos. At a time when distinguishing photography from generated imagery is getting increasingly difficult, that’s a surprisingly meaningful feature.
Watch out: You can’t simply sell unmodified Pexels images as standalone stock, and recognizable people, logos, brands, and artwork can involve rights beyond the photographer’s copyright.
Pixabay is the Swiss Army knife of free media sites.
While Unsplash and Pexels built their reputations primarily around photography, Pixabay contains photos, illustrations, vectors, video, GIFs, music, sound effects, and other media. That makes it especially useful when a project suddenly requires an icon, background illustration, or looping video five minutes before delivery.
Its Content License permits broad commercial use and modification, subject to restrictions such as selling content on a standalone basis.
Best for: Mixed-media projects, illustrations, backgrounds, vectors, video, presentations.
Why designers should care: You can potentially source several different types of assets without bouncing between six websites.
AI warning: Unlike Unsplash and Pexels, Pixabay accepts AI-generated content and has guidelines specifically covering it. So if human-made imagery is the goal, check the individual asset and contributor rather than assuming everything was captured through a lens.
Kaboompics is one of those resources that makes you wonder why it isn’t mentioned more often.
Created by photographer Karolina Grabowska, it has a particularly strong collection of interiors, lifestyle, fashion, beauty, food, and aesthetically styled scenes. The big advantage is that many images come from coordinated photoshoots rather than unrelated contributors.
That means you can often find several photographs of the same scene, model, or environment from different angles.
Best for: Branding, fashion, beauty, interiors, editorial layouts, social campaigns.
Why designers should care: Visual consistency. Instead of stitching together five photographs with completely different lighting and color temperatures, you can build a page from one coherent shoot.
Even better, Kaboompics explicitly says its photographs come from real photoshoots and aren’t AI-generated. It also provides complementary color palettes for images, which is the kind of detail that suggests somebody remembered designers would actually be using these things.
Watch out: Check individual licensing information, particularly if an image is identified for editorial rather than broader commercial use.
StockSnap takes a refreshingly straightforward approach to free photography.
Its library includes business, nature, people, food, travel, technology, and abstract imagery, with photographs released under CC0. That gives designers broad copyright permission to copy, modify, and use images commercially without attribution.
The interface is also relatively uncluttered, making StockSnap a good second search when the giant libraries are producing too much noise.
Best for: Business websites, technology, nature, lifestyle, backgrounds.
Why designers should care: CC0 is about as painless as copyright licensing gets, and there’s a decent selection of images with enough negative space for headlines and interface elements.
Simple. Free. Photographic. Sometimes that’s precisely what you need.
Burst was created by Shopify with businesses and entrepreneurs in mind, and that commercial DNA shows.
Instead of focusing exclusively on artistic landscapes and impossibly attractive people wandering through Iceland, Burst has imagery around products, retail, entrepreneurs, workplaces, fashion, fitness, food, and e-commerce.
That makes it particularly useful when you’re designing something that actually needs to sell something.
Best for: E-commerce stores, startup sites, product categories, marketing campaigns, small businesses.
Why designers should care: The photography often anticipates real commercial layouts. You’ll find images that work naturally as category banners, website headers, marketing backgrounds, and product-related content.
It’s stock photography that occasionally remembers the website needs to convert.
picjumbo has one of the better origin stories in stock photography.
Designer and photographer Viktor Hanacek launched the site after stock agencies rejected his photographs for “lack of quality.” Millions of downloads later, that assessment has aged about as well as you’d expect.
The collection includes business, technology, food, fashion, people, nature, and lifestyle photography, with free images available for commercial projects, including client work.
Best for: Web design, blogs, app mockups, marketing, lifestyle brands.
Why designers should care: Because much of the library comes from a recognizable photographic voice, it often feels less like an anonymous stock warehouse and more like browsing a photographer’s portfolio.
There are premium options too, but the free collection is substantial enough to deserve a bookmark.
Watch out: picjumbo notes that some images may not have model or property releases. That’s particularly important for advertising and other uses involving recognizable people, brands, or properties.
NegativeSpace offers high-resolution photography across categories including architecture, technology, people, nature, objects, and workspaces.
The collection is smaller than the major stock giants, but that’s not necessarily a disadvantage. Searching Unsplash for something like “office” can feel like being asked to choose one grain of sand from an entire beach.
Smaller collections can be faster.
Best for: Architecture, objects, technology, workspaces, abstract backgrounds.
Why designers should care: NegativeSpace makes its photographs available under CC0, giving designers broad freedom to use and adapt them commercially without attribution.
It’s particularly useful as a secondary source when you’ve started seeing the same familiar stock images everywhere else.
And finding a photograph that hasn’t already appeared on 38 SaaS landing pages is increasingly a design feature in itself.
Life of Pix is a photography-focused library featuring high-resolution work from photographers around the world.
The collection leans toward expressive, editorial-style photography rather than aggressively optimized commercial stock. You’ll find architecture, landscapes, street photography, food, textures, objects, and unusual compositions.
Best for: Editorial layouts, architecture, travel, food, atmospheric hero imagery.
Why designers should care: Life of Pix is useful when you want a photograph to establish a mood rather than merely illustrate the noun in your headline.
It feels more like browsing photography and less like searching a database for “businesswoman pointing upward.”
As with any free-stock source, check the current license and any restrictions associated with the image before using it in higher-risk commercial applications.
Gratisography has spent years doing almost the exact opposite of conventional stock photography.
Photographer Ryan McGuire built the collection around strange poses, exaggerated expressions, surreal concepts, bright colors, and images with actual personality.
If your search for “business meeting” keeps producing eight people applauding a pie chart, Gratisography may be the antidote.
Best for: Humorous campaigns, editorial graphics, unconventional brands, social media.
Why designers should care: Weird is useful. In a world where enormous image libraries are converging on the same polished visual language, intentionally odd photography can make a design instantly more memorable.
Gratisography permits commercial use under its license, subject to its restrictions.
Not every client will approve.
That’s partly the point.
Public Domain Pictures is less polished than most modern stock sites, but that’s also why it’s useful.
The collection contains a sprawling mixture of photography, illustrations, textures, backgrounds, historical material, and other images contributed over many years. Much of the site’s material is presented as public domain, making it useful when you need something more specific—or simply less familiar—than what the fashionable stock libraries keep returning.
Best for: Backgrounds, textures, nature, objects, illustrations, unusual subjects.
Why designers should care: It has the chaotic charm of the older web. You may have to dig, but that digging can surface images that haven’t been optimized into the same aesthetic as every contemporary stock collection.
Watch out: Check the information attached to the individual image. Public-domain copyright status doesn’t automatically resolve model releases, trademarks, property rights, or other third-party restrictions.
ISO Republic offers free photography and video across popular categories such as technology, business, architecture, food, nature, and people.
The collection is straightforward and practical, which makes it particularly useful for everyday production work rather than endless inspiration browsing.
Its imagery is offered under CC0, allowing commercial use without attribution.
Best for: Websites, blogs, presentations, backgrounds, commercial marketing.
Why designers should care: Sometimes you don’t need a revolutionary photograph.
You need a good photograph of a desk by 4 PM.
ISO Republic is exactly the kind of secondary library worth keeping around for those searches.
Skitterphoto was created by photographers and built around freely usable photography.
Its collection covers nature, architecture, people, animals, transportation, objects, travel, and plenty of scenes that don’t necessarily feel engineered around stock-photo keywords.
Images are published under CC0, providing broad commercial reuse rights without mandatory attribution.
Best for: Nature, cities, everyday objects, travel, editorial design.
Why designers should care: The site’s photographic roots make it an appealing place to hunt for images that retain a little more of the photographer’s personality.
When you’re specifically trying to escape the increasingly synthetic look of online imagery, that imperfection can be exactly what you’re looking for.
Foodiesfeed solves one problem extremely well: food.
You’ll find ingredients, restaurant dishes, coffee, desserts, cooking scenes, healthy food, fast food, and all the beautifully lit carbohydrates your layout can handle.
For designers working on restaurant sites, recipe projects, delivery apps, food blogs, or packaging mockups, a specialist library can produce far better results than searching a generic stock site.
Best for: Restaurants, menus, delivery apps, recipes, food brands, hospitality.
Why designers should care: Food photography is difficult to fake convincingly—and bad food photography can make a perfectly good restaurant look like a crime scene.
AI warning: Foodiesfeed contains AI-generated imagery as well as conventional photography. If authentic human-made photography is specifically required, check the individual image before downloading it.
New Old Stock is where this list starts becoming much more interesting.
Rather than contemporary stock photography, it curates vintage photographs from public archives and material presented as having no known copyright restrictions.
The result is an extraordinary mixture of old cities, transportation, workplaces, people, industrial scenes, travel, and everyday life.
Best for: Retro branding, editorial design, historical projects, collage, moodboards.
Why designers should care: Historical photography gives designs instant texture and character. It also avoids one increasingly common problem with modern stock: everything looking suspiciously like everything else.
Nobody in these photographs is pretending to brainstorm around a laptop.
Mostly because the laptop hasn’t been invented yet.
Important: Don’t treat New Old Stock itself as automatic commercial clearance. The site advises users to follow through to the originating institution and check its rights statement when determining whether commercial use is permitted. Think of it as an excellent discovery tool for historical imagery, then verify the individual source.
Rawpixel’s Public Domain collection is a treasure chest for designers who think beyond photography.
The important words here are Public Domain collection. Rawpixel itself contains other types of content—including AI imagery—but this specific collection brings together public-domain material from museums, libraries, and historical archives.
You’ll find botanical illustrations, paintings, engravings, Japanese prints, scientific drawings, maps, decorative motifs, vintage advertisements, and much more.
Best for: Collage, packaging, editorial design, posters, branding, vintage aesthetics.
Why designers should care: These assets can become ingredients rather than finished images. Crop them, isolate elements, combine several pieces, add typography, recolor them, or use tiny fragments as textures.
Before asking an image generator for “an authentic 19th-century botanical engraving,” remember that the 19th century already made quite a lot of them.
The Metropolitan Museum of Art has made hundreds of thousands of images of public-domain artworks available through its Open Access initiative.
And “artworks” barely describes the range involved. Paintings, sculpture, armor, textiles, furniture, photography, manuscripts, ceramics, jewelry, musical instruments, and objects spanning thousands of years are searchable.
Look for objects identified as Public Domain/Open Access rather than assuming every image on the museum’s website has identical rights.
Best for: Fine art, editorial layouts, cultural projects, patterns, textures, collage.
Why designers should care: This isn’t a stock library created to anticipate what marketers might search for. It’s human visual culture stretching across civilizations.
That unpredictability is precisely what makes it useful.
A 2,000-year-old decorative motif may provide a more interesting starting point for your next identity project than page 19 of “abstract gradient background.”
The Rijksmuseum in Amsterdam has been remarkably progressive about making its collection accessible digitally.
Its online resources include high-quality reproductions of paintings, drawings, prints, historical objects, decorative arts, and photography, including masterpieces by Rembrandt and Vermeer.
But don’t just search the famous names. The real design gold can be hidden in old book illustrations, ornamental objects, textiles, engravings, and tiny details buried inside paintings.
Best for: Fine art, textures, historical imagery, editorial design, collage.
Why designers should care: High-resolution museum imagery can be cropped aggressively. A tiny corner of a 17th-century painting can become an extraordinary full-screen texture or background.
A large amount of the Rijksmuseum’s digitized public-domain material is available for reuse, but check the copyright or rights statement attached to the individual object rather than assuming every image in the entire collection is unrestricted.
Rembrandt probably never expected his work to appear behind a newsletter signup form.
Design evolves.
Smithsonian Open Access is almost absurdly large.
Millions of digital items from Smithsonian museums, libraries, archives, research centers, and other collections are available, with millions designated CC0.
That means photographs, scientific specimens, historical objects, artworks, aircraft, animals, inventions, documents, clothing, cultural artifacts, and things sufficiently strange that no conventional stock photographer would ever think to shoot them.
Best for: Science, history, nature, technology, editorial design, experimental projects.
Why designers should care: Specificity.
Generic stock photography is good at “person using computer.”
Smithsonian collections are good at “prototype flying machine from 1898.”
Guess which one readers are more likely to remember.
Look specifically for the CC0 designation when you want material from its Open Access program.
The National Gallery of Art provides tens of thousands of Open Access images of works it believes to be in the public domain, available for commercial and non-commercial use.
The collection includes paintings, drawings, prints, photographs, sculpture, and decorative arts from centuries of art history.
High-resolution reproductions make the archive useful not merely for displaying entire paintings but for extracting details, textures, palettes, compositions, and visual references.
Best for: Editorial design, art projects, cultural content, backgrounds, moodboards.
Why designers should care: Fine art is an astonishingly underused source of design material considering how much of it is freely accessible.
Your client asked for something that feels sophisticated and premium?
Apparently, a Renaissance master fits the budget.
The Library of Congress is one of the web’s great visual rabbit holes—but commercial-use rights need a little attention.
Its enormous collections contain historical photography, posters, maps, architectural drawings, advertisements, newspapers, manuscripts, illustrations, and documentation of everyday life across generations.
The easiest starting point is its Free to Use and Reuse portal, which groups material the Library believes is public domain, has no known copyright, or has been cleared for public use.
Best for: Editorial design, history, collage, maps, Americana, research-heavy projects.
Why designers should care: Search specificity is the superpower. You can find imagery no conventional stock library would have any commercial reason to maintain.
Watch out: Don’t assume everything on the entire Library of Congress website is commercially unrestricted. For material outside its Free to Use and Reuse collections, check the individual Rights & Access statement.
NASA’s Image and Video Library may have the highest production value of any free image resource on Earth.
Technically, some of it wasn’t even photographed on Earth.
Search missions, planets, astronauts, rockets, satellites, Earth observation, the Moon, nebulae, spacecraft, mission control, and decades of space exploration.
Best for: Editorial and informational projects involving science, technology, space, history, and futuristic interfaces.
Why designers should care: NASA imagery is genuinely difficult to replicate because NASA has access to a rather exclusive photography studio called space.
And because so much of the imagery documents real missions and real objects, it has an authenticity that even spectacular generated space art doesn’t possess.
Watch out: Don’t translate “NASA imagery is often reusable” into “NASA says I can do absolutely anything with everything on its website.” NASA has specific media guidelines, particularly around advertising and promotional uses, its protected insignia and logos, identifiable people and employees, implied endorsement, and third-party copyrighted material.
For commercial advertising, merchandise, or promotional use, check NASA’s current media guidelines before proceeding.
Wikimedia Commons is simultaneously one of the most useful and least glamorous websites a designer can bookmark.
It contains tens of millions of photographs, illustrations, maps, diagrams, artworks, flags, historical images, scientific material, and media covering almost every conceivable subject.
Need a photograph of a specific railway station in Romania? A medieval astronomical diagram? A 1920s vacuum cleaner?
There’s a decent chance somebody uploaded it.
Best for: Places, history, obscure objects, science, diagrams, editorial content.
Why designers should care: Wikimedia Commons excels precisely where commercial stock fails: subjects too specific, old, obscure, academic, or unprofitable for a stock photographer to care about.
The catch: There isn’t one universal Wikimedia license. Every file has its own licensing information. Public-domain and CC0 files are usually simplest; CC BY requires attribution, while CC BY-SA adds share-alike requirements.
Read the file page before using the image.
Yes, we know. Reading again.
Openverse isn’t really another image library.
It’s a search engine for openly licensed media, which potentially makes it more powerful than many individual sources on this list.
Instead of limiting yourself to one archive, Openverse searches Creative Commons and openly licensed material from numerous sources. Filters can help narrow results by license and commercial-use permissions.
Best for: Highly specific subjects, Creative Commons photography, research, unusual imagery.
Why designers should care: Openverse is what you use when searching the big stock libraries for 20 minutes has produced absolutely nothing useful.
It expands the search beyond conventional stock and into the enormous ecosystem of openly licensed photography, museum material, archives, and other collections.
Watch out: Treat Openverse as the discovery layer, not the final licensing authority. Openverse itself recommends verifying licensing information. Follow the result to its original source and check the current license before using it commercially.
Yes, Flickr still exists.
More importantly, its age has accidentally become one of its greatest advantages.
Flickr contains an enormous photographic archive created over decades, including vast amounts of material uploaded long before generative AI existed. You’ll find travel photography, street scenes, events, architecture, hobbies, communities, technology, nature, and the wonderfully mundane details of everyday life.
Filter for Creative Commons licenses that allow commercial use, such as CC BY or CC BY-SA, and it becomes an extremely powerful source.
Best for: Travel, cities, events, street photography, niche subjects, authentic everyday imagery.
Why designers should care: Flickr photographs frequently look like photographs, rather than stock photography.
They’re sometimes imperfectly composed. Lighting isn’t always immaculate. Random people wander into the background. Things look used. Cities look messy.
In 2026, those imperfections can feel surprisingly refreshing.
Watch out: Flickr hosts images under many different licenses. Check each photograph individually and follow its attribution and other licensing requirements.
If your goal is specifically to find human-made imagery, there’s an extremely simple technique:
Search the past.
A photograph uploaded to Flickr in 2009 wasn’t generated by Midjourney. A museum scan of an 1887 botanical illustration wasn’t created by Stable Diffusion. A NASA photograph from Apollo 11 didn’t begin with somebody typing “cinematic astronaut, dramatic lighting, ultra detailed, 8K.”
Older Creative Commons collections, historical archives, digitized museum collections, and established photography libraries contain an enormous reservoir of unquestionably human-created imagery.
And there’s another advantage.
It’s often more visually interesting.
Contemporary stock photography has become extraordinarily polished. So has AI imagery. Both can converge toward the same immaculate lighting, perfect composition, flawless skin, carefully curated interiors, and suspiciously photogenic bowls of salad.
Historical and documentary imagery wasn’t created to satisfy today’s visual algorithms.
That’s exactly why it can make a design feel different.
Museum and archival resources become particularly interesting when designers stop treating them as finished photographs.
Download a high-resolution public-domain painting and look at it at 400%.
Suddenly you have:
A 500-year-old painting doesn’t have to sit politely inside a rectangular frame with the artist’s name underneath it.
It can become raw design material.
The same applies to maps, scientific diagrams, botanical illustrations, vintage advertisements, NASA imagery, and historical photography.
That’s where these archives can become dramatically more interesting than ordinary stock sites.
Here’s the boring section that could potentially save you from a much more exciting email from a lawyer.
Copyright permission and commercial clearance aren’t always the same thing.
An image may be free to reproduce under CC0 or another permissive license while containing other protected elements.
A photograph might include a recognizable person with publicity or privacy rights. A building might contain protected artwork. A product could display a trademark. A photograph of an artwork could involve multiple layers of rights depending on the artwork, photograph, jurisdiction, and intended use.
This becomes particularly important when imagery is used in advertising, product packaging, merchandise, political campaigns, endorsements, sensitive subjects, or anything suggesting that a recognizable person supports a company or product.
For an editorial illustration accompanying a blog post, the risk profile can be very different from printing someone’s face across 50,000 shampoo bottles.
When the commercial stakes are high, check the individual asset and, where necessary, get appropriate legal guidance.
If you’re going beyond the big stock libraries, you’ll encounter Creative Commons licenses constantly.
The simplified designer version is:
CC0 — The copyright owner has waived copyright and related rights to the extent legally possible. It’s usually the easiest starting point for commercial design, but it doesn’t automatically clear trademarks, privacy or publicity rights, recognizable people, or other third-party rights.
CC BY — Commercial use is permitted, but you must provide appropriate attribution and comply with the license conditions.
CC BY-SA — Commercial use is permitted with attribution, but adaptations generally need to be distributed under the same license.
CC BY-NC — The NC means NonCommercial. Don’t use it for commercial work unless you separately obtain permission.
That’s deliberately simplified; Creative Commons licenses contain more detail than four lines in a design article can cover.
When in doubt, click the license.
Don’t assume this article—or any stock-site roundup—is the final licensing authority forever. Websites change owners. Terms get rewritten. Collections are reorganized. Contributors upload material under different licenses. Platforms introduce AI content. Yesterday’s completely free library becomes tomorrow’s “start your seven-day trial.”
Before using an asset commercially, check the current license on the actual source. If the project matters, keeping a record of the applicable license and source information at the time you downloaded the image is also a sensible habit. It takes 20 seconds. Twenty seconds is considerably cheaper than legal correspondence.
AI has given designers something extraordinary: the ability to generate virtually any image they can describe. Strangely, that may make images we can’t perfectly control more valuable.
The crooked street photograph. The grainy image from 1974. The strangely cropped Flickr upload. The scratches on an old NASA photograph. The uneven ink in a botanical print. The cracked paint on a Renaissance portrait. The random person wandering into the background of somebody’s photograph from 2008.
These used to be imperfections. Now they’re evidence. Evidence that somebody stood somewhere, looked at something, decided it was worth recording, and pressed a shutter—or picked up a pencil, brush, engraving tool, or camera.
AI imagery isn’t going anywhere, nor should it.
But when everyone has access to an infinite supply of perfectly generated images, authenticity becomes a design material of its own.
And remarkably, there’s still an enormous amount of it available for free.

                                    Apple iPhone Duo vs. Xiaomi 18 Fold: Software matters
                                

                                    Apple iPhone Duo vs. Samsung Galaxy Z Fold 8: It comes down to just one thing
                                

                                    Forget the iPhone Ultra — I went hands-on with the Xiaomi 18 Fold, and it might be the best mid-size foldable you can't buy
                                
Get the latest news from Android Central, your trusted companion in the world of Android
You are now subscribed
Your newsletter sign-up was successful
Samsung saw an upward trend on home soil.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

It's just over a week since Apple's main event where it debuted the iPhone Duo, its first foldable phone. Trends have shifted since then, and it's Samsung that's seeing an uptick in Fold 8 sales.
The Chosun (Korean) reported that Apple's event provided a noteworthy boost in Galaxy Z Fold 8 sales following the iPhone Duo's debut (via GSMArena). This appears to be the result of consumers ""waiting and seeing,"" as the publication notes. According to the report, Samsung observed a 10% boost in Galaxy Z Fold 8 sales following the iPhone Duo reveal.
There might've been a few deciding factors that consumers were waiting out. The report highlights the price difference between the two phones as one of them. That price might be minimal in the U.S. (the iPhone Duo is $100 more), but in South Korea, there's a one million won difference. What's more, the report acknowledges that there is no 1TB option for the Fold 8; however, if consumers wanted the 2TB iPhone Duo, they'd have to pay five million won (by comparison, the Fold 8 starts at 2.2 million won).
These two factors were cited as the deciding factors for Korean consumers when choosing between the Fold 8 and the iPhone Duo. The publication went on to highlight one more factor: actually receiving the phone. Consumers can more readily go out and purchase a Fold 8. But the iPhone Duo isn't set to appear until October. In the U.S., Apple said pre-orders begin October 16, but its market debut isn't until October 23.
 



It was nice finally seeing Apple's long-awaited foldable debut. Shortly after the event, Android Central's Harish Jonnalagadda was quick with his two cents about it. While an appealing phone, there were some glaring differences between it and the competition. Comparing the iPhone Duo's weight was highlighted. Both Samsung's Galaxy Z Fold 8 and Google's Pixel 11 Pro Fold are lighter (it's 53g heavier than the Fold 8).
Elsewhere, Samsung and, yes, Duolingo, chirped Apple on social media during the event. It's probably safe to say Samsung wasn't too impressed. It said, ""Making us wait? Fine. We'll be over here, folding 3 ways."" The company even said the iPhone Duo's bedside clock mode was ""giving groundhog day."" Then we have Duolingo—completely unhinged. Its post read, ""They named a phone after me and made it bend over,"" accompanied by its mascot's—you get the idea.
Getting back on topic, the Galaxy Z Fold 8 was already incredibly popular following its debut. After a week of pre-orders, the Fold 8 shattered expectations with its high volume. Inventory sold out online, and the Fold 8 far outpaced the Fold 8 Ultra and Flip 8.
Get the latest news from Android Central, your trusted companion in the world of Android
I'm sure this is the case for a lot of people. The ""waiting and see"" approach is how to stay smart in a market that demands a lot from your wallet. But also, Samsung and Apple are major competitors. People might grab one phone one year and then the other the next. It's a constant flow. An ever-shifting reality we live in. However, it speaks a little more volume on Samsung's end. Clearly, people were on the fence and wanted to see. But after seeing, they still opted for Samsung. That's good for now. I want to know if it'll stick, though.
Nickolas is always excited about tech and getting his hands on it. Writing for him can vary from delivering the latest tech story to scribbling in his journal. When Nickolas isn't hitting a story, he's often grinding away at a game or chilling with a book in his hand.

Activision Blizzardcandy crush sagaKinglabor 
At least two Swedish unions have issued a strike notice against Candy Crush Saga developer (and the “K” in ABK) King. In a post on LinkedIn, Unionen, Sweden’s largest trade union, wrote, “Our members at King are entitled to secure conditions, a say in their workplace and clear rules that apply to everyone. That is why Unionen is giving notice of strike action.”
In Sweden, unions operate on a kind of two-tier system. Workers can individually join a trade union that represents and bargains for their entire profession at any time. They can also form smaller unions, called union clubs, that are exclusive to a specific workplace. At King, the members of its union club have been in negotiations for a collective bargaining agreement (CBA) for over a year. In June, King executives ultimately refused workers’ requests, saying the company’s “current benefits offering is better for our employees” and that its “current operating model is the right fit for King, including our operations in Sweden, at this time.”
It is because of this denial that Unionen and the Swedish Engineers unions are calling for a strike. “A collective agreement is about more than pay. It covers pensions, insurance, working hours and the ability to influence conditions at work. It is a cornerstone of security in the Swedish labour market.”
According to a press release from the Swedish Engineers union, arbiters are working to mediate a solution. But if no agreement cannot be reached by September 25th, workers will go on strike.
“A strike is not something we take lightly,” Swedish Engineers wrote in its press release. “But the signal from our members at King is that they want us to take the next step to secure their conditions and influence.”
Update Thursday September 17th 3:50 PM ET: In an email to Kotaku, Timo Rybak, ombudsman of Unionen has confirmed that three more Swedish unions have issued strike notices to King: AkademikerfÃ¶rbundet SSR, Naturvetarna and DIK.

20,2026-09-17,"The number of people applying for unemployment benefits dropped sharply last week, another sign that layoffs remain rare and most Americans enjoy job security
WASHINGTON -- The number of people applying for unemployment benefits dropped sharply last week, another sign that layoffs remain rare and most Americans enjoy job security.
The Labor Department reported Thursday that jobless claims slid to 196,000, the fewest since mid-July and down from 206,000 the week before. The four-week average of claims, which smooths out week-to-week volatility, dropped to 203,250.
Economists had expected claims to come in at 207,500, according to a survey by the data firm FactSet.
Claims for jobless benefits are a proxy for layoffs, and economists watch them because they can be a sign of where the job market is headed. For the past year, claims have mostly stayed within a historically low range of 200,000 to 230,000 a week.
The American job market has remained sturdy despite higher gasoline prices that have squeezed businesses and consumers since the fighting with Iran began Feb. 28.
Layoffs are low. Businesses, remembering the labor shortages that followed the end of pandemic lockdowns, are reluctant to let go of staff. They’re hiring — but modestly by the standards of recent years.
So far this year, employers — companies, government agencies and nonprofits — have been adding an average 80,000 jobs a month, including a surprising 162,000 in August. That is an improvement on a dismal 2025 when monthly job creation averaged 9,700 as high interest rates and President Donald Trump’s ever-changing trade policies discouraged hiring.
But it remains well below the 166,000 monthly jobs created, on average, in 2023 and 2024, and the 491,000 a month recorded during the 2021-2022 hiring boom that followed COVID-19 lockdowns.

21,2026-09-17,"Google won't have to break up its ad adtech empire, but there's a long list of changes it will have to make after being found liable for illegally monopolizing certain online ad markets.
The federal judge overseeing Google's landmark adtech antitrust trial has now unsealed her ruling outlining the behavioral remedies the ad giant will face.
The key takeaway is that Google — which operates an ad server that publishers use to manage and sell ads, the buying tools that help marketers buy ads, and an exchange that connects the two — won't be able to give its own services preferential treatment in auctions for ads that appear on websites across the open internet. The remedies will apply globally for six years.
""We're very pleased the court rejected the DOJ's proposal to break apart tools that help small businesses reach new customers and grow,"" a Google spokesperson said earlier this month when the judge presiding over the case announced she wouldn't order a breakup of the company
Here's a plain-English translation of the key remedies and what they mean for the various players in the online ad market.
Translation: Google can't force publishers using its DFP ad server to also use its AdX exchange, or use auction mechanisms that give AdX an advantage over rivals.
First Look gave AdX the first chance to bid on an ad impression, while Last Look let it see what rival exchanges were bidding before deciding to place its own bid.
Patrick Briggs, CEO of digital ad agency Semify, previously HubShout, said removing advantages such as Last Look could make ad auctions more trustworthy, potentially improving the value of the broader display ad market.
Translation: Google's exchange and publisher ad server must work with Prebid, the widely used open-source technology that lets publishers invite multiple ad exchanges to bid for their ad space using a technique called header bidding. Google was a longtime holdout.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
Rajeev Goel, CEO of adtech company PubMatic, said the remedy should help rival exchanges compete more fairly with AdX. But if Google's integration works differently from Prebid's existing connections with other exchanges, it could create friction.
""It's kind of like when all of your devices take USB and then somebody comes out with a new device and a different type of charger,"" Goel said. ""You're like, 'Oh man, now I've got to put all these new types of chargers in all the rooms in my house, in my office, and in my car,' and it creates a lot of wasted effort.""
Goel testified as a witness for the Department of Justice in the trial.
Translation: Publishers should be able to receive bids from advertisers through AdX without also having to use Google's DFP ad server.
Goel said this should allow publishers to judge whether DFP is ""the best possible ad server for me on the merits of the ad server, not the merits of the ad server plus the AdX monetization.""
Wyatt Fore, a partner at the law firm Shinder Cantor Lerner, said interoperability requirements have a long history in antitrust enforcement, pointing to a 1968 FCC ruling that required AT&T to allow rival devices to connect to its telephone network.
Translation: Publishers need to be able to take their data with them if they switch to a rival ad server.
""It reduces switching costs,"" said Nick Stoltz, chief strategy officer of the media-measurement company Measured.
Rob Retzlaff, executive director of the Connected Commerce Council, said smaller publishers may welcome more competition but will also want to ensure changes don't disrupt the tools they rely on for revenue. Google is a corporate partner of the small-business advocacy group.
Translation: AdX can't disadvantage publishers for using rival adtech, while DFP can't favor an ad simply because the winning bid came from Google.
Stoltz said Google's presence on both the buying and selling sides of online advertising had created an opaque marketplace that disadvantaged publishers. He said the remedies should ""create a fairer market and put the other sell-side platforms on a more even playing field"" with Google.
Translation: Google can't recreate its Google-only bidding pipeline another way. Google Ads can still prioritize AdX when doing so would produce better results for advertisers — because, for example, it offers better fraud or privacy protections — just not because Google owns it.
Goel said the principle should be that Google's buying tools allocate advertisers' money based on where they'll get the best return, rather than funneling it toward Google's own products.
Translation: A court-appointed monitor, paid for by Google, will police its compliance and can inspect Google's source code, algorithms, and documents, and interview employees.
Fore said independent monitors can be particularly useful in complex technology cases, where companies may technically comply with an order without fulfilling its broader intent.
""I kind of wish that more judges would appoint independent monitors for complex cases like this, where there will be a million disputes about whether or not Google is complying with the order,"" Fore said.
While the judge has decided the remedies, the final legal order spelling out exactly how they will work hasn't yet been issued. Google has said it plans to appeal part of the court's liability ruling.
