62,2026-09-17,"During the Age of Sail, it might be weeks or even months before a ship was able to return to dry land and its crew was able to replenish their supplies. 
As a result, a shipâs supplies were often well stocked with anything and everything that could withstand the long journey: bread, salted meat, butter, cheese, and ale (which kept longer than fresh water) were all common staples. Contrary to popular belief, however, naval ships were often kept rather well equipped for long sea journeys, thanks to an ever-improving knowledge of safe preservation techniques, and a global network of supply ports and naval bases at which ships could safely dock for days at a time before continuing their journey. Pirate ships, however, didnât have quite the same luxury.Â 
As they operated on the wrong side of the law, pirates couldnât exactly sail into a port any time they needed to, and as a result, their onboard supplies werenât quite so well stocked, nor able to be quite so frequently replenished. 
As if life as a pirate were not hard or dangerous enough, ultimately, the food on a pirateâs plate was often basic, bland, and just on the wrong side of fresh.Â 
Naval âbreadâ in the Age of Sail was more often than not served up in the form of a dense, dry, flour-and-water dough biscuit known as hard tack. Although not exactly fresh nor flavorful, the tack nonetheless kept longer than ordinary breadâalthough after months at sea, even it would still typically end up infested with weevils.Â 
Depending on where they were setting out from, at the start of a journey ships were sometimes able to stock up with simple livestock, such as chickens and goats, that, unlike larger livestock like sheep and cows, would typically eat whatever was given to them, and were therefore easier to maintain. (Pirates, meanwhile, might be able to plunder livestock from the ships they attacked.) Keeping chickens onboard therefore guaranteed a supply of eggs, while goats could supply fresh milkâbut as the supplies on board understandably dwindled, the livestock themselves would often end up on the menu as desperate times called for desperate measures. Nonetheless, as unwise as eating your only source of fresh produce might sound, at least their meat was fresh.Â Â 
Salting and drying meat was certainly one way of keeping it fresh for longer periods of time, ensuring that a shipâs crew had a nutritious and filling source of protein regardless of how far from land they were. The quality of the meat couldnât always be guaranteed, however, and onboard pirate ships in particular it was often particularly grim. There are even reports of shipâs beef being so tough that the crew found it more useful as a means of making belts and strapsâwhereas the meat supplies of one crew, that of buccaneer Henry Morgan in 1670, became so ran down that they resorted to slicing up and frying a leather bag.Â 
As pirates often patrolled the same waters, they had the advantage of getting to know the produce available naturally in their region, which in the tropics and Caribbean often included fresh fruits. Although these didnât keep so well onboard, a ship could nonetheless stock up on fresh produce every now and then, ensuring at least a few daysâ supply of the likes of coconuts and avocados, andâafter they were introduced to plantations from Southeast Asiaâthe likes of bananas and mangos. The English buccaneer and explorer William Dampier is even credited with the earliest English-language recipe for guacamole, written in 1697.Â 
As it turns out, the popular image of a rum-swilling pirate is not too far from the truth. Fresh water could quickly turn stagnant and spoil on board a ship, whereas alcohol could be kept far longer. And in the Caribbean, rumâwhich could be made cheaply, quickly, and plentifully from the sugarcane introduced from Southeast Asiaâsoon became the preferred drink of choice. Pirates could easily stock up their own supplies, or else plunder it from the merchant ships that carried an immense supply of Caribbean rum back and forth across the Atlantic during the Golden Age.Â 
Spending a long time at sea, and unable to safely restock in large ports patrolled by garrisons of soldiers, made restocking supplies on board pirate ships decidedly tricky. As a result, many shipsâ crews had to improvise, and ultimately added anything and everything they could get their hands on to their menus. In his logs and diaries, for instance, the aforementioned buccaneer William Dampier recorded trying flamingo, armadillo, locusts, ostrich eggs, sea lion kangaroo, and even Galapagos tortoises during his voyages. You might not be quite so keen to try those today, but you can at least thank Dampier for introducing a handful of tastier dishes to our dining tables, as his works include the earliest records of not only guacamole, but tortilla chips and soy sauce too.Â

After starting a company in one of the most heavily regulated sectors, I’ve learned that lasting success isn’t driven by innovation alone.
Copied to clipboard

		Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.	
When people think of the industries shaping the future of business, property management probably isn’t at the top of the list. Most entrepreneurs likely don’t think they have much to learn from the housing industry. But after two decades spent building software for landlords and property managers, I’ve found that it is one of the best places to learn what it really takes to build a lasting company. 
Housing is one of the most regulated, fragmented and emotionally charged industries in the U.S. Businesses are navigating an ever-changing mix of federal, state and local regulations, shifting economic conditions, evolving customer expectations and emerging technologies — all while earning and maintaining the trust of customers making high-stakes financial decisions every day. Success requires more than a great product or an innovative idea. It demands resilience, adaptability and a long-term commitment to solving real problems.
Building a business in this environment has taught me lessons that extend far beyond property management. Whether you’re building a SaaS platform, launching a consumer brand or growing a professional services firm, today’s business landscape is defined by one constant: change. The lessons I’ve learned from this industry are surprisingly universal. They’re not just about rental housing — they’re about building a business that can adapt, endure and grow no matter what comes next. 
Startup culture celebrates innovation — breakthrough ideas, disruptive products and companies that set out to transform an industry. Innovation is essential to entrepreneurship; it’s often what starts companies, creates opportunity and gets attention. But innovation alone doesn’t build a company that lasts — adaptability does. 
My software company wasn’t built around one disruptive idea. It was built on firsthand experience with a real-world problem I knew others were struggling with too. Over time, we’ve continued to evolve our software alongside our customers’ changing needs. We haven’t sustained growth for two decades because we were always the first to innovate — we’ve grown because we prioritize listening, learning and adapting. 
Property management, like many industries, is defined by constant change. We can’t always predict what’s coming next, but we can control how we respond when challenges arise. The Covid-19 pandemic was a powerful reminder that adaptability matters. Businesses across industries were forced to rethink how they operated, served customers and delivered value. For us, that meant rapidly developing new features and providing resources to help customers navigate changing rental regulations. The companies that adjusted quickly were best positioned to navigate uncertainty, while those unable to change struggled to keep pace.
On a less revolutionary scale, the same principle applies to day-to-day operations. Changing compliance requirements, evolving cybersecurity expectations and shifting customer needs all require the same approach: listen closely, understand the challenge and adapt. 
The lesson is simple: Innovation may help you get noticed, but adaptability helps you endure. The companies that build lasting advantages aren’t the ones constantly chasing the next big thing — they’re the ones continuing to evolve as the world around them changes.
It’s easy to get caught up in what competitors are doing or what new technology makes possible. I’ve learned that the best products are built by understanding the problems customers actually face. 
It starts with customer empathy. You have to understand the challenges your customers navigate, the decisions they make every day and the obstacles standing in their way. Building the right solution requires understanding their reality. Our product has evolved by staying close to our customers and responding to their changing needs. Every feature, improvement and update is guided by a simple principle: Does this create value for our customers? How can we make their jobs easier, more efficient and more successful? 
The key is to create meaningful opportunities for customers to share input — through one-on-one conversations, support interactions, surveys, social channels or other touchpoints where customers can share ideas, challenges and frustrations. The most important step? Turning those insights into action. Even as CEO, I regularly connect with customers who share feedback, suggest improvements and help us identify opportunities to build a better product. That’s market research you can’t get anywhere else.
In complex industries, the strongest competitive advantage comes from knowing your customers better than anyone else. Your customers should shape your strategy. 
Markets fluctuate, regulations change and technology evolves. The companies that last are those that build relationships and credibility strong enough to weather the shifts.
In the housing industry, trust is everything. Property managers are responsible for people’s homes, financial transactions and sensitive personal information. In the grand scheme of things, the longest feature list or the most impressive marketing claims matters less than having a trusted partner they can rely on every day. 
Building trust takes time. It comes from consistently delivering value, supporting customers when new challenges arise and continuing to invest in their success long after the initial sale. A company can win new customers with a great product, but it retains them through reliability, transparency and results. This long-term vision has helped us build lasting relationships with our customers — many of our software clients have been users for years and years, a reflection of the trust we’ve earned by continuing to support their businesses through the ups and downs. 
Short-term growth can create momentum, but long-term trust creates staying power. When your customers have confidence in your team and your product, they’re more likely to continue growing with you through whatever comes next. 
America’s housing challenges aren’t going away anytime soon, and neither is uncertainty in business. Successful entrepreneurs view complexity as an opportunity instead of an obstacle. The industries with the most complicated challenges are often the ones with the greatest need for thoughtful solutions and businesses ready to invest the time to understand them. 
Building a business in a complex industry has taught me that lasting success comes from embracing complexity. It requires staying curious, adapting when circumstances change and continuing to solve meaningful problems for the people you serve.
When people think of the industries shaping the future of business, property management probably isn’t at the top of the list. Most entrepreneurs likely don’t think they have much to learn from the housing industry. But after two decades spent building software for landlords and property managers, I’ve found that it is one of the best places to learn what it really takes to build a lasting company. 
Housing is one of the most regulated, fragmented and emotionally charged industries in the U.S. Businesses are navigating an ever-changing mix of federal, state and local regulations, shifting economic conditions, evolving customer expectations and emerging technologies — all while earning and maintaining the trust of customers making high-stakes financial decisions every day. Success requires more than a great product or an innovative idea. It demands resilience, adaptability and a long-term commitment to solving real problems.
Building a business in this environment has taught me lessons that extend far beyond property management. Whether you’re building a SaaS platform, launching a consumer brand or growing a professional services firm, today’s business landscape is defined by one constant: change. The lessons I’ve learned from this industry are surprisingly universal. They’re not just about rental housing — they’re about building a business that can adapt, endure and grow no matter what comes next.

64,2026-09-17,"You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?
A practical, honest framework for CEOs who are off plan heading into Q4 — and don’t want to carry the same problems into next year.
Copied to clipboard

		Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.	
Over the years, I’ve seen many CEOs make a common mistake heading into Q4. Instead of taking action, they wait. They tell themselves an underperforming program or leader will work itself out before the calendar switches to January. That almost never happens. CEOs who push out hard decisions invariably find themselves in the same difficult situation in the coming year.
Every quarter has its own nuances and characteristics. This Q4 carries extra weight. The noise around AI is deafening, and CEOs who keep kicking that strategy down the road will regret it. 
Vistage’s recent research found that 84% of small and midsize U.S. businesses have already started using generative AI, and 76% of CEOs use it themselves. But only 22% have a governance plan behind it. Wages are also ticking back up, making it more expensive to retain great people. On top of that, customers increasingly want clear justification and communication of the value companies are delivering, especially amid increased prices.
The following are five time-tested strategies I’ve seen effective CEOs use to run a Q4 course correction.
The first move is the hardest: Stop waiting. If months of data have clearly shown that a program, product or person isn’t working, the right answer is almost always to stop investing further. Don’t give it one more quarter.
This is the “sunk cost fallacy” at work. We tell ourselves we’ve put in too much to walk away now. But that time and money are gone either way. The only question that matters is whether continuing will produce a different result. If not, the best CEOs shut it down.
That also applies to opportunities. Many of the best leaders spread themselves across too many initiatives, hoping one breaks through. But focusing on the handful of things that genuinely move the needle creates the clarity a team needs to execute.
Every course correction meeting I’ve seen work well includes clearly articulated customer stories: real outcomes customers are receiving from a company’s product or service. 
Doing a better job for existing customers — and winning more like them — is the catalyst that energizes a team and points everyone toward the one path that can reliably get a business back on track.
“Course correct” assumes leaders already know what isn’t working. Most CEOs do not, at least not at first. It’s usually a combination of things, and untangling them requires a carefully planned process.
Great CEOs start by making sure everyone understands the overarching strategy and how their objectives connect to it. From there, confirm people are committing to the activities the plan requires. Then, check for resources: Do teams have the skills and tools they need to execute?
When CEOs are confident that they’ve addressed these key issues, they can drive results by making sure objectives are met and plans are implemented. Now that the “productivity machine” is operating optimally, it will be much easier to tell if there really are market issues or macroeconomic issues impacting the business. These can be addressed through a strategy modification — but only if the productivity machine is still working at full speed.
Every CEO knows surprises are coming. Almost none of us plans well for them. But planning for surprise can be the difference between implementing an effective, well-timed course correction — and missing an opportunity.
The first discipline of being prepared for surprise is trust. Great leaders trust their teams to hit their objectives when unexpected challenges arise. When surprises turn into excuses and accountability gets diffused, a downward spiral begins that’s hard to reverse.
The second discipline is staying anchored. Pivots typically won’t be dramatic if every decision connects back to the company’s mission, vision, purpose and values. On the other hand, if each surprise causes questions at the very roots of the company, leaders end up like a tumbleweed tossed around by whatever wind blows through next.
The best mindset going into a course correction is fact-based and ego-less. It’s tempting for leaders to believe they designed the perfect strategy and the team simply failed to execute it. The truth is usually somewhere in between. Knowing when something isn’t working — and shifting away without taking it personally — is one of the most valuable skills a CEO can develop.
It’s also why experienced CEOs lean on a peer group or a coach to uncover blind spots. An outside perspective, from someone with no stake in the original idea, makes a better diagnosis possible.
Course correction is not a sign the year failed. It is a practice the best CEOs build into every fourth quarter. Leaders who run this sprint now turn this year’s misses into next year’s advantage.
Over the years, I’ve seen many CEOs make a common mistake heading into Q4. Instead of taking action, they wait. They tell themselves an underperforming program or leader will work itself out before the calendar switches to January. That almost never happens. CEOs who push out hard decisions invariably find themselves in the same difficult situation in the coming year.
Every quarter has its own nuances and characteristics. This Q4 carries extra weight. The noise around AI is deafening, and CEOs who keep kicking that strategy down the road will regret it. 
Vistage’s recent research found that 84% of small and midsize U.S. businesses have already started using generative AI, and 76% of CEOs use it themselves. But only 22% have a governance plan behind it. Wages are also ticking back up, making it more expensive to retain great people. On top of that, customers increasingly want clear justification and communication of the value companies are delivering, especially amid increased prices.

65,2026-09-17,"Reports of Intel and SK Hynix teaming up to build memory chips in Ohio sent stocks surging across the sector, but the most revealing signal of the day has nothing to do with either company and everything to do with…
 Market Movers desk. Editor: David Moadel.
Read More: Learn 7 secret wealth tips high net worth investors use that most investors miss (sponsor)
This post may contain links from our sponsors and affiliates, and Flywheel Publishing may receive compensation for actions taken through them.
Memory chip stocks are rallying Thursday morning after reports that Intel and SK Hynix are in early discussions about producing memory chips together on U.S. soil for the first time. The Roundhill Memory ETF (CBOE:DRAM) is up 4% to $57.67, suggesting a sector-wide response rather than a single-name repricing.
Intel (NASDAQ:INTC | INTC Price Prediction) stock is up 8% to $109.03 in Thursday morning trading. The move reflects a market bet that an anchor tenant is what finally turns Intel’s idle Ohio complex into a revenue line. For a broad tech-market contrast, the Invesco QQQ Trust (NASDAQ:QQQ) up 1.55% to $715.66.
Meanwhile, SK Hynix (NASDAQ:SKHY) stock is up 5% to $183.57. The Korean memory leader already dominates high-bandwidth memory, so a U.S. fab reads as insurance against tariffs and customer pressure rather than transformation for SK Hynix.
Reuters reported on Wednesday, citing people familiar with the discussions, that SK Hynix and Intel are weighing two structures: a lease of part of Intel’s Ohio chipmaking complex, or a joint venture with Intel and major cloud computing firms to lock in memory supply. Neither company has confirmed a deal.
SK Hynix said it’s exploring options to strengthen global competitiveness and that nothing has been finalized. Intel declined to comment on what it called speculation, while restating its commitment to preparing the Ohio site. The two statements leave room for the talks to advance or fade.
Ohio’s project was announced as the world’s largest chipmaking complex, and its fabs have slipped years past the original schedule, leaving committed capital sitting idle. A tenant filling Ohio capacity would convert an idle plant into a revenue line for Intel. That’s the shift the Ohio angle is really about, and it’s why the reported talks moved Intel stock more than they moved SK Hynix stock.
SK Hynix already leads in high-bandwidth memory and has American assembly capacity coming in Indiana. However, fabricating memory in the United States costs more than assembling it, and one gating risk for SK Hynix sits in Seoul rather than in Ohio. Advanced memory can be classified in South Korea as national core technology, which triggers a government review of any transfer abroad.
Micron Technology (NASDAQ:MU) stock is up 6% to $979.25. Micron is the only large memory maker headquartered in the United States, and federal subsidies under the CHIPS Act were built around that position.
If SK Hynix fabricates memory on American soil, Micron’s domestic advantage narrows, and the CHIPS Act premium built into the story starts to look less exclusive. Yet, Micron stock is rising more than SK Hynix stock today, which suggests the market is pricing memory scarcity and stronger group pricing ahead of any competitive consequence for Micron specifically.
That tension is the day’s most interesting signal for anyone watching the Intel and SK Hynix headline. The reported talks aren’t straightforwardly bullish for Micron, and traders are treating them as bullish because the underlying story is memory supply that can’t keep up with artificial intelligence demand. Scarcity trumps market share when both are pushing prices at once.
The bull case for Intel rests on a customer rather than on a product. One complication is that nothing has been confirmed by either party, and early talks about two different structures are not a signed agreement. Investors can watch for whether either Intel or SK Hynix moves past the “speculation” posture with a formal statement.
The South Korean national core technology review is the other clock hanging over any Intel and SK Hynix arrangement. Shareholders may want to keep an eye on whether Seoul signals its stance, since that process could delay a deal regardless of what Intel and SK Hynix agree between themselves.
Given how much of today’s move in Intel and SK Hynix is priced off an unconfirmed deal, investors should keep their positions sized for headline risk in either direction. A denial from Seoul or a competitive response defending Micron’s CHIPS Act footprint could unwind part of the rally quickly, and neither Intel nor SK Hynix has given the market a firm milestone to anchor to yet. Sizing exposure to Intel or SK Hynix on today’s move means accepting that the next headline could just as easily walk the story back as advance it.

There's not much the American people agree on these days. From Trump Steaks to plastic straws, from vaccines to Teslas, it's hard to pinpoint a topic that hasn't been polarized by politics. But a series of new surveys suggest Americans may have finally found some common ground, at least for the moment: growing concerns about artificial intelligence.
Last week, a long-running industry debate about AI safety broke containment and went viral after an Anthropic researcher named Jacob Coxon quit his job and revealed that ""the people building AI earnestly believe that it could kill us all by the end of the decade."" Soon, dozens of lawmakers had responded with calls to regulate AI — and the top executives of the major artificial intelligence companies had all come out in favor of a global slowdown in AI development.
But as polls show, Americans would be fine with that. A full 63% — including 70% of those who voted for former Vice President Kamala Harris in 2024 and 60% of those who voted for President Trump — believe there is at least a ""moderate"" risk that ""advanced AI will destroy humanity,"" according to the latest Politico poll. Only 7% say there is ""no risk at all"" of an AI apocalypse. Thirty-seven percent say the risk is ""significant"" or ""almost certain."" 
This is wild. (I would have said ""very small risk."") https://t.co/Mkxj5ifPw5 pic.twitter.com/zXAcFx4ybQ
— Timothy B. Lee (@binarybits) September 17, 2026
The same survey, which was conducted from Sept. 13 to 15, also found that nearly half of U.S. adults (48%) think ""AI is now good enough, and we should pause the development of more advanced models to reduce any risks coming from them"" — while nearly a third (31%) believe ""we should continue developing more advanced AI because [new models] could still bring significant benefits."" 
As AI continues to dominate the stock market, Trump has insisted in recent days that additional guardrails are unnecessary and that those who say otherwise are ""Conspiracy Theorists, Treasonists, Traitors, and Leakers."" 
""The only control or 'guardrails' that AI needs is a STRONG AND SMART (High IQ!) PRESIDENT, and the U.S.A. has that, in spades!"" he wrote on social media. 
Yet Americans seem to see things differently. According to a snap YouGov survey conducted on Tuesday, 28% now say they approve of how the president is handling the issue of artificial intelligence. About twice as many (55%) say they disapprove. 
""President Trump is in one galaxy, and the American people are in a completely other galaxy,"" CNN's Chief Data Analyst Harry Enten told viewers on Monday. ""They're just simply not anywhere close together on this one.""
Sep 14-15 poll of 3,564 U.S. adults (±1.9 points)Do you approve or disapprove of the way Donald Trump is handling AI?28% of U.S. adults approve55% disapprove, including 44% who strongly disapprove(Link in reply) pic.twitter.com/7KpzpuATOb
— YouGov America (@YouGovAmerica) September 15, 2026
Earlier this week, YouGov asked 18,238 U.S. adults to say how concerned they are, if at all, ""about the possibility that AI will cause the end of the human race on Earth?"" Half said ""very"" (23%) or ""somewhat"" (27%); fewer said ""not very"" (21%) or ""not at all"" (17%). 
These fears are not new. In fact, they were festering even before Coxon kickstarted the current wave of existential panic. Last November, Yahoo partnered with YouGov to poll on artificial intelligence. At the time, 10% of Americans predicted that AI's impact on humanity would turn out to be mostly positive; while (47%) expected it to be mostly negative. 
To find out why, Yahoo and YouGov asked Americans to read four ""predictions about AI"" and say ""how likely"" it is that each ""will come true at some point in the future."" 
Here's how they responded:
61% of Americans said it was somewhat or very likely that ""AI will continue to improve, but it will never surpass human intelligence.""
64% of Americans said it was somewhat or very likely that ""AI will be able to perform any intellectual task a human can.""
60% of Americans said it was somewhat or very likely that ""AI will become so intellectually advanced that it will help us solve complex problems like curing cancer and stopping climate change.""
63% of Americans said it was somewhat or very likely that ""AI will become so intellectually advanced that humans won't be able to control it anymore.""
And 53% of Americans say it's somewhat or very likely that ""AI will destroy humanity."" 
In other words, roughly six in 10 Americans said they believed that AI would someday exceed human capacity — and human control.
It's not just potential AI extermination that worries Americans. Most simply don't feel that the technology's upsides outweigh its downsides — at least not yet. 
According to the new Politico poll, for example, just 19% of Americans think the development of AI has benefitted them personally. In contrast, the vast majority say AI's impact on them has either been neutral (49%) or negative (16%).
The Politico poll also asked Americans if they would be comfortable with AI being used for various cutting-edge tasks. Across the board, majorities said no: ""to provide an initial diagnosis for medical symptoms"" (51% uncomfortable); ""to track people's movements to aid police in finding criminals"" (52% uncomfortable); ""to grade children's homework"" (53%); ""to manage administrative tasks in the government"" (59% uncomfortable); ""to make predictions about who is likely to commit a crime"" (62% uncomfortable); and ""to make judgments on who is guilty or innocent in a court of law"" (70% uncomfortable). 
Ultimately, 25% of U.S. adults think AI will create more jobs than it replaces over the next decade, according to the Politico poll. As a result, even more Americans say they're concerned about ""the possibility that AI will cause mass unemployment"" (73%) than say they're concerned about AI ending humanity (50%), according to YouGov.
Meanwhile, seven in 10 Americans say they are afraid AI will weaken human bonds, according to a poll released this week by Elon University, and about six in 10 say AI's potential to take over most human tasks will undermine an individual's sense of purpose. Most American adults also think AI is going to do more harm than good in education, according to this month's NBC News Decision Desk Poll — while a growing share say schools that shun the technology will help students more than schools that embrace it.
So it's no surprise, in the end, that two-thirds of Americans (67%) believe that AI is ""advancing too quickly"" — versus just 21% who believe it's progressing at ""about the right pace."" For most U.S. adults, the risk-reward ratio of unfettered AI just isn't adding up.
Data centers have existed in some form since the dawn of the computer age. Until recently, most Americans didn't have a strong opinion about them. 
AI has changed that. To keep up with the technology's massive processing demands, thousands of new data centers are being built all over the country — especially in rural areas. They tend to be much bigger and more powerful than older data centers.
The climate-focused media outlet Heatmap has been gathering public opinion on data centers for the last year. In August 2025, Americans were divided on whether they would support (43%) or oppose (42%) a ""new data center being built near where you live."" Now, just twelve months later, support has cratered to 15% — and a full three-quarters of Americans (75%) say they would oppose data center construction in their communities. More than six in 10 (61%) say they would ""strongly oppose"" it (up from 24% a year ago). 
We've got exclusive new polling on local data center development at @heatmap_news. Over the past year, we've asked Americans whether they would support or oppose a data center being built near where they live. We haven't changed the wording. When we first polled the question… pic.twitter.com/G1LCfuriMw
— Robinson Meyer (@robinsonmeyer) August 20, 2026
Heatmap's executive editor has described this as ""a swift, massive shift in US public opinion on a scale that I wouldn't have once thought possible.""
The shift is being driven, in part, by concerns about water and electricity usage as well as increases in noise, heat and the disruptions of construction. According to a new Associated Press poll, a majority of Americans (53%) now say they are ""extremely"" or ""very"" concerned about artificial intelligence's environmental impacts, up from 41% last year. 
But broader skepticism about AI is also playing a role; as Yahoo recently explained, ""it's a tough sell to ask people to tolerate the downsides of having a data center in their community when they don't want the products that the facilities are being built to support."" As a result, the new AP poll found that about 6 in 10 Americans now want to limit the number of new data centers that can be constructed — including most Democrats and Republicans. 
That bipartisan backlash is key. Among Harris voters, 70% say there's at least a ""moderate risk"" of AI destroying humanity, according to the Politico poll, versus just 22% who say there's ""no risk"" or a ""very small risk."" 
The numbers among Trump voters? 61% and 29%, respectively. 
That's still a clear majority. And almost exactly the same number of Trump voters say AI is advancing too quickly (60%) rather than at about the right pace (29%), according to YouGov. 
This sort of consensus is rare. Even rarer is the fact that political figures on both sides of the aisle are publicly saying very similar things about AI. On Tuesday, both Vermont Sen. Bernie Sanders (""the country's elder democratic socialist"") and former Trump advisor Steve Bannon (""a leading architect of the MAGA movement"") spoke at a ""Pro-Human Assembly"" in Washington, D.C. — and both of them ""urged Americans to demand tighter restrictions on artificial intelligence before its capabilities exceed human control,"" according to the New York Times. 
""When you are racing towards a cliff, you hit the brakes,"" Sanders said. ""We are at a dangerous and unprecedented moment in world history. And it is imperative for all of us to stand up.""
""American citizens are not going to be supplicants to the oligarchs anymore,"" Bannon added. ""This is a hinge in history.""
On Wednesday, data journalist Nate Silver pointed out that while ""it's been widely predicted that AI would devolve into a highly partisan issue once it hit the mainstream"" — and while ""there's certainly been a lot of vitriol on Twitter"" — this ""conspicuously hasn't been true in the polling so far.""
In fact, neither party seems to have an advantage on the issue. According to a new New York Times / Siena College poll released this week, 42% of voters say they trust Republicans to do a better job handling AI — while basically the same number (40%) say they trust Democrats. 
Whether the current AI consensus can last long enough for Congress to pass substantive regulation remains to be seen. Inside the White House, there has been a ""quiet freakout"" over AI policy, according to a new report in the Wall Street Journal, with a group of senior aides gathering ""almost daily to discuss risks in the sector and potential guardrails… while trying to persuade Trump to take a more hands-on approach."" 
""So far, the president seems unpersuaded. ""AI, and Data Centers, will be the Greatest Economic Development Engine in History — Bigger than Oil, Gold, Diamonds, or even the Internet,"" Trump wrote Monday on social media. ""It will not be stopped by brilliantly run Destructive Forces during the Term of President DONALD J. TRUMP!""
But YouGov polled that post, too. Just 26% of Americans said they agreed — and more than twice as many (57%) said they disagreed.

69,2026-09-17,"Oracle just reported the loudest cloud quarter in enterprise software history, yet the stock keeps sliding. Here is why that disconnect may be the most asymmetric setup in the market right now.
 Price Targets desk. Editor: Vandita Jadeja.


Oracle just posted the loudest cloud quarter in enterprise software history, and the stock is falling anyway. That mismatch is the whole story.
Oracle (NYSE:ORCL | ORCL Price Prediction) trades at $147.17 as of the September 17, 2026 open, down 51.46% over the past year despite an AI backlog that now dwarfs any other software company’s. Our 24/7 Wall St. price target for Oracle is $214.69, implying roughly 50% upside over the next 12 months. Our recommendation is buy, with 90% confidence.
Oracle’s fiscal Q1 2027, reported September 10, 2026, was the kind of earnings report that used to trigger multi-day rallies. Revenue hit $19.345 billion, up 29.6% year over year, non-GAAP EPS came in at $1.92 versus a $1.74 estimate, and cloud infrastructure grew 121%.
Remaining performance obligations swelled to $664 billion, with more than $30 billion in new AI contracts booked in the quarter alone.
Yet shares have fallen 8.95% in the past week, extending a five-session slide that MarketWatch flagged for missing the broader AI bounce, news that dovetailed with a surprising Larry Ellison announcement covered by Barron’s. The market is fixating on the negative $5.396 billion quarterly free cash flow and $28.499 billion capex bill while discounting the backlog.
Bulls have a specific, quantifiable thesis. Half of the $664 billion RPO is expected to convert to revenue over the next 36 months, and management guided full fiscal 2027 revenue to at least $90 billion with non-GAAP EPS of $8.10.
GPU utilization at 97.9% and renewals resold at a 20% premium point to real pricing power. The consensus analyst target of $239, with 8 Strong Buys and 28 Buys, backs that view. Our bull scenario projects $332.25 if Abilene, Shackleford, and Vera Rubin systems all ship on schedule.
The bear thesis is a cash math problem. FY26 free cash flow was negative $23.686 billion, interest expense climbed 55% to $1.4 billion, and full-year FY27 capex is guided to $90 to $95 billion. Any slip in Abilene, New Mexico, or Wisconsin data-center delivery would push RPO conversion out.
Bulls counter that CFO Hilary Maxson described these as “strong free-cash-flow-generating” projects once ramped, with roughly 100% conversion of post-tax EBITDA. Our bear scenario floor sits at $181.20, still above today’s price.
The most direct comparison is against the two hyperscalers Oracle is actively taking share from through its multicloud footprint. Microsoft (NASDAQ:MSFT) trades at a forward P/E of 26 and a price-to-sales ratio of 11, with an analyst target of $572.92.
Microsoft’s Azure remains the anchor hyperscaler and now co-hosts Oracle databases inside its regions, which makes it both a rival and a partner. Oracle’s forward P/E of 17 is a full multiple turn cheaper on growth that is faster in the cloud segment.
Alphabet (NASDAQ:GOOGL) trades at a forward P/E of 22 and a price-to-sales ratio of 9. Google Cloud is Oracle’s most direct multicloud partner and its clearest AI infrastructure rival.
Alphabet’s 54.8% profit margin dwarfs Oracle’s 26.4%, but Oracle’s IaaS is growing at 121%. On both peer benchmarks, Oracle looks cheap, which makes our $214.69 target look conservative rather than aggressive.
Our 24/7 Wall St. price target of $214.69, buy rating, and 90% confidence rest on one factor: the RPO backlog is too large and the multiple is too low for this to stay disconnected for long.
The bull case works for investors who can tolerate volatility and expect Oracle to convert even 40% of RPO into revenue over three years. The bear case gains weight if the FY27 capex guide of $90 to $95 billion forces another equity raise beyond the completed $20 billion ATM program. The setup favors patient buyers.
These projections assume Oracle continues converting its AI backlog and returns to positive free cash flow by fiscal 2029. Significant upside or downside could result from data-center delivery timing or a sudden shift in AI compute pricing. The buildout Oracle is financing has to be powered, cooled, and networked by somebody, and we profiled seven of those suppliers in a free report on the AI infrastructure trade.

70,2026-09-17,"Laptops have finally gotten fast enough to be true desktop replacements. The one problem is that no laptop can connect to everything on your desk. A MacBook might only give you two or three USB-C ports, so you are going to need a dock.
So I got the iVANKY FusionDock Ultra, which as far as I know is the most expensive and most powerful dock currently on the market. It is $649. I want to explain what makes it special, and then I want to talk almost all of you out of buying one.
 
When I bought my first MacBook Pro and started shopping for hubs, I could not understand the price range. The answer is bandwidth, and it is the single most important thing to understand before you spend a dollar on a dock.
If you are plugging in a keyboard, a mouse, or a printer, none of this matters. Those devices will work exactly the same over a $6 cable. The trouble starts with the expensive peripherals: external SSDs, a fast network connection, and the big one, monitors. You might own a 4K display, but a cheap cable or a cheap dock can quietly limit it to 1080p. You might own a 120 Hz panel that is being fed 60 Hz, or even 30. macOS will not warn you that any of this is happening, and in some cases there is no place in the operating system to even look it up. You end up blaming your laptop for feeling slow when the real problem was the $30 hub sitting behind your monitor.
Start with your own laptop. Apple has changed port speeds almost every year, sometimes putting different speeds on different ports of the same machine, so there is no clean rule I can give you here. The vast majority of Macs in use right now have USB-C ports that move data at 40 Gb/s. With the M4 and M5 generations, Apple moved to Thunderbolt 5, which looks identical but doubles that to 80 Gb/s. The fastest way to find out what you have is to type your exact laptop model into an AI chatbot and ask.
Then add up your monitors, because they are what actually eat the bandwidth, and almost nobody accounts for them:
Look at that last number. A single 10-bit HDR display consumes essentially an entire 40 Gb/s port on its own. My own desk runs four 4K 60 Hz monitors, which is 52 Gb/s, plus a 10-gigabit Ethernet adapter, which puts me around 62 Gb/s. That leaves roughly 18 Gb/s to share among every other thing I plug in, and honestly, it has been plenty. I have some of the fastest SSDs on the market and they still copy at full speed.
Two things, really. The first is that this dock connects to your Mac with two upstream Thunderbolt 5 cables instead of one. They are magnetic, so they snap together into what feels like a single cable spaced exactly right for a MacBook's two ports, and they pull apart and stack vertically if you want to run the dock off something like a Mac Studio. iVANKY rates the dock at up to 120 Gb/s in its boost mode, and the practical result is that it can drive four 6K 60 Hz displays natively, which very few docks on the planet can do. There is a DisplayPort 2.1 output, HDMI, four downstream Thunderbolt 5 ports, UHS-II SD and microSD slots, and 140 W of charging back to the laptop.
The second thing is the one I actually care about: 10-gigabit Ethernet is built in. Right now I am using an external Thunderbolt to 10-gigabit adapter that cost me over $200, is about half the size of this entire dock, and gets hot enough that you do not want to touch it. I pulled a huge file off my Synology NAS through the Ultra's built-in port and got 750 MB/s, which is exactly the 10-gigabit speed I was getting from the separate adapter. That adapter is now in a drawer.
My current laptop is an M3 Max MacBook Pro. It does not even have Thunderbolt 5. This dock is completely overkill for the machine I plugged it into, and I knew that before I ordered it. I bought it because I am about to buy the next Apple laptop the moment it ships, and I am about to replace my monitors with better ones, and at that point I will blow past what my older dock can carry. I am buying for a setup I do not own yet.
You almost certainly should not do that. Work backwards instead. Figure out how fast your ports are, count your monitors, note their resolution and refresh rate, and see how much bandwidth is left for everything else. If you are running one or two ordinary 4K 60 Hz displays, a far cheaper dock will serve you perfectly and you will never notice a difference. If you want three or four displays, or high refresh rates, or anything past 4K, that is when the expensive tier starts to make sense. The FusionDock Max 1 is $299.99 and the newer FusionDock Max 2 is $399.99, and both cover that ground. The Ultra's real separation from those two is the 10-gigabit Ethernet and the fourth 6K display, and if neither of those is in your future, you are spending a few hundred dollars for nothing.
I have been using an older iVANKY dock every single day for years and it has been one of my favorite pieces of gear, so I recommend the brand without hesitation. I just do not recommend this particular model to most people.
The speed readout I used to check those file transfers is not something macOS gives you. It comes from Better Dock, an app I built that replaces the macOS Dock. Among a lot of other things, it keeps file transfer dialogs pinned where you can see them instead of losing them behind a window, and it shows you the actual transfer speed while a copy is running. For anyone who cares whether their cables, card readers, and docks are performing the way they are supposed to, that turned out to be the feature I use constantly. It is free while it is in beta at betterdockapp.com.
 
Lee Morris is a professional photographer based in Charleston SC, and is the co-owner of Fstoppers.com"

73,2026-09-17,"Amazon just handed Generac a contract that sent shares surging, but buried inside the deal is a warrant arrangement that could let the customer claw back the very gains it created. Here is what the peer group silence from Cummins…
 Market Movers desk. Editor: David Moadel.
Read More: Learn 7 secret wealth tips high net worth investors use that most investors miss (sponsor)
This post may contain links from our sponsors and affiliates, and Flywheel Publishing may receive compensation for actions taken through them.
Generac Holdings (NYSE:GNRC | GNRC Price Prediction) stock is up 16% to $203.76 in Thursday morning trading after the backup-power maker announced a long-term agreement to supply generators for hyperscale data centers. Amazon (NASDAQ:AMZN) is the counterparty on the multi-year deal, and the tech giant also received warrants in Generac as part of the arrangement. That combination of a headline revenue win and equity issuance to the customer is the piece the tape has to price this morning.
Sector participation in the Generac stock rally is notably thin. The Industrial Select Sector SPDR ETF (NYSEARCA:XLI) is up 0.1% to $168.89, essentially flat on the session. Meanwhile, the SPDR S&P 500 ETF Trust (NYSE ARCA:SPY) is up 0.9% to $760.8, and the broad market is doing more of the day’s work than industrials.
Caterpillar (NYSE:CAT) stock is up 2% to $799.43 as a shared data-center-power narrative gives it a modest lift, though that move is a fraction of what Generac is putting up. Cummins (NYSE:CMI) stock, the closest listed comparable on standby-generator work for hyperscale customers, is unchanged at $527.48, and the absence of a move is the clearest tell that today’s action with Generac stock is one-company specific rather than a category repricing.
Generac’s signed agreement with Amazon is reported at $2.4 billion, and it locks in multi-year volumes of backup generators for Amazon’s data center footprint. That kind of contract reframes Generac from a consumer standby-power brand best known for residential home generators into a hyperscaler-grade infrastructure supplier, which typically earns a different valuation multiple in most sell-side models.
The complication for Generac is the warrant grant. Amazon receiving equity in Generac as part of the arrangement means the customer captures part of the economics its own orders create, and that dilution has to be weighed against the revenue win. So far, the revenue side appears to be winning that argument in the price action, but the warrant math is the piece that may get scrutinized as the initial headline energy around Generac fades (we lined up seven other names powering the AI data center buildout, from power to cooling, in a free report here).
A separate figure is doing a lot of the emotional work in the Generac stock rally. Canaccord Genuity has flagged that the Amazon relationship could eventually unlock up to $8 billion in orders for Generac, well above the $2.4 billion of business the two sides have actually signed. Sell-side ceiling numbers travel quickly in a session like this one.
That $8 billion is a sell-side potential, not a commitment, and the distance between the contracted amount and the analyst’s ceiling is the entire argument on Generac stock, according to Canaccord Genuity. The bull case prices the Amazon relationship as the anchor account that opens the door to further hyperscaler wins. Skeptics can note that the signed number is what actually shows up on Generac’s invoice, and the rest is a possibility rather than a booking.
The peer read-across matters here for framing what the market is actually pricing. Cummins also builds standby power systems for data centers and would be the natural beneficiary of a broader repricing of hyperscaler backup demand, yet Cummins is unchanged on the session while Generac runs. Caterpillar’s move is participation, not confirmation, and the XLI is barely off the flat line.
For Generac, the message from the industrial complex is that today’s move is a single-customer, single-supplier event rather than a category repricing of standby power. That is rotation into one name, not a sector-wide verdict on backup generation. It also frames the risk for anyone chasing Generac Holdings on the assumption that peers will follow later in the session.
Traders may want to keep an eye on whether Generac Holdings holds today’s gains once the initial headline flow fades and the warrant math gets a closer read from the sell-side desks. The stock is running on a customer-conversion story, and follow-through depends on whether a second hyperscaler contract or an order revision from Amazon lands in the coming quarters.
Investors can size their positions in Generac stock with the warrant dilution in mind, since a customer that owns equity in its supplier changes the math on future pricing negotiations. A moderate position in Generac shares, rather than a chase at the highs of the session, keeps their exposure in line with what has actually been contracted versus what is still an analyst projection of what could arrive later.

81,2026-09-17,"Jim Cramer just called Nokia a strong buy, which would be great news except for one uncomfortable fact that long-suffering NOK shareholders know all too well about his track record.

Long-suffering Nokia shareholders finally got the CNBC blessing this week. On the September 16 Lightning Round of Mad Money, Jim Cramer told a caller asking about Nokia (NYSE:NOK | NOK Price Prediction) that “I like Nokia very much… I think it’s a terrific situation and I would be a buyer right here, right now.” The endorsement came a day before Nokia disclosed an expanded partnership with Microsoft (NASDAQ:MSFT) to launch an AI-powered telecom network automation platform integrating Nokia Data Suite with Microsoft Fabric, sending shares up 3.8% intraday Thursday.
Here is why some Nokia holders may cringe at the timing. A cottage industry of “Inverse Cramer” strategies has grown up around the premise that fading his calls beats following them. Quiver Quantitative runs an Inverse Cramer portfolio, and the popular CramerTracker account on X recently circulated three-year performance numbers claiming the inverse strategy returned 171.6% versus 146.6% for a Nancy Pelosi tracker. One viral post joked that “Inverse Cramer being autopilot’s best performing fund will never not be hilarious.”
Nokia has moved well beyond its sleepy value-trap reputation. The ADR is up 132.81% over the past 12 months and 65.14% year to date, trading around $10.53 after touching a 52-week high of $17.36. Market capitalization now sits near $58.95 billion. That rally reflects a genuine business inflection under CEO Justin Hotard, who has retooled the Finnish equipment maker around what he calls the AI supercycle.
The Q2 2026 report on July 23 gave the bulls their ammunition. Revenue of $5.49 billion beat estimates by 13.79% and EPS of $0.0799 beat the $0.0682 consensus. Most striking, AI and Cloud customer revenue more than doubled year over year to $508.96 million, with order intake of EUR 2.8 billion in the segment. Optical Networks grew 19% and IP Networks grew 15% on a constant currency basis. Details are in the 6-K filing.
Hotard told analysts on the call, “If there was more supply, I think we’d probably generate more revenue,” underscoring that supply capacity is the binding constraint. Nokia is addressing that by acquiring NXP’s Chandler, Arizona semiconductor fabrication campus for Indium Phosphide production, ramping a new San Jose optical fab in Q4, and boosting Pennsylvania test capacity tenfold.
Nokia carries a trailing P/E of 70 and a forward P/E of 22, with a PEG of 0.818 and an analyst consensus target of $14.96. Wall Street sentiment is mixed: four strong buys, four buys, two holds, and one strong sell.
The complicating footnotes matter. Reported Q2 operating income was a loss of $57 million after $445.05 million in accelerated restructuring charges. Free cash flow ran negative $835 million, and net income dropped 94.44% year over year. Management expects EUR 800 million in total restructuring charges in 2026 as it integrates its China operations and simplifies its European footprint.
The Microsoft news reported by Barron’s and the WSJ Thursday morning extends a strategic pattern that includes the prior Nscale networking partnership and a patent agreement with Amazon. AI-RAN pilots are on track to begin at year-end, with commercial availability in 2027. For Q3, Nokia guided sequential net sales growth of 3% to 7%. Cramer’s viewers should watch order conversion. Management expects about half of AI and Cloud orders to convert to revenue within twelve months. Whether the Inverse Cramer crowd or the CNBC faithful ends up looking smart on NOK will hinge on that conversion metric.

The deal was first announced in September of 2025, and now that itâs finally happened, many workers and fans are anticipating mass layoffs as EA seeks to service $18 billion in debt, with the publisher telling debt investors that it would cut $170 million in costs through new âorganizational efficiencies,â according to reporting from Bloomberg. 83,2026-09-17,"
The word “compromise” gets thrown around a lot in tiny home conversations — and for good reason. Most builds force you to choose between a proper kitchen or a real bedroom, decent storage or actual floor space. JT Collective’s Summit refuses that trade-off entirely. Built on a quad-axle trailer and stretching 37 feet in length, this family-owned California builder’s flagship model is one of the more thoughtfully resolved tiny homes on wheels the market has seen in a while.
At 400 square feet, the Summit sits at the generous end of the tiny home spectrum without crossing into pretension. The exterior wears a combination of wood and dark metal paneling — a pairing that reads rugged and considered at once, the kind of facade that doesn’t beg for attention but earns it. Dual roof pitches break up what could have been a monotonous roofline, giving the structure genuine architectural character rather than the boxy silhouette that plagues so many THOW builds.
Designer: JT Collective







Inside, the layout centers around livability. Large trifold glass doors dissolve the boundary between the living room and the outdoors — a move that, on a square-footage-constrained floor plan, is less a design flourish and more a spatial strategy. Natural light pours in through dual-pane, fully tempered windows, and the open living area is generous enough to hold a proper sofa without feeling like a showroom floor model arrangement. The kitchen comes standard with a stove, hood vent, and refrigerator, with washer and dryer connections already roughed in — details that signal JT Collective is building for people who actually intend to live here, not just weekend in it.
The single bedroom comes with a dedicated closet, which sounds like a small thing until you’ve toured a dozen tiny homes that treat clothing storage as an afterthought. The bathroom includes a double vanity — an inclusion that shifts the Summit from aspirational novelty to practical daily driver. Starting at $179,000, it positions itself in a crowded segment, but the build quality and spatial intelligence justify the ask.







What JT Collective has built with the Summit isn’t just a capable tiny home — it’s an argument that downsizing doesn’t have to mean settling. The proportions are right, the material palette is restrained in the best possible way, and the floor plan reflects the kind of real-world thinking that usually gets designed out in favor of renderings that look good in a gallery. This one looks just as good lived in.

86,2026-09-17,"As a tech nerd who has tracked fitness gear for years, I have seen watches evolve from simple counters into complex computers. The smartwatch market often forces you to choose between a stylish daily companion and a hardcore fitness tool. Fortunately, the team at Suunto has been working hard to change that narrative with their latest sports watch releases.
I have spent some quality time looking at the brand new Suunto Run 2. This device is built for runners and serious athletes who want premium features without jumping to a massive outdoor watch. Suunto put itself back on the radar with the original Run, and this new model adds some massive upgrades that make competing brands take notice. Here is my deep dive into how the Suunto Run 2 performs in the real world.
When you first unbox the Suunto Run 2, you notice how lightweight and comfortable it feels on your wrist. The watch features a 46-millimeter polymer case paired with a sleek stainless-steel bezel that gives it a premium look. Up front sits a vibrant 1.32-inch AMOLED touchscreen display that hits a peak brightness of two thousand nits. That display brightness makes reading your workout metrics effortless even under direct sunlight.
The watch weighs just 54 grams with the included silicone strap, making it easy to wear all day without feeling bulky. Controls are handled through a physical three-button layout on the right side of the case, featuring a smooth digital scroll wheel in the center. Suunto offers the watch in two distinct hardware configurations based on internal storage. You can pick up a four-gigabyte base model or opt for a massive 64GB version if you want maximum room for maps and music files.
Both storage versions share the exact same physical build, featuring quick release straps that are simple to swap out whenever you want a fresh style. The silicone band is stretchy and secure, giving athletes a reliable fit during fast interval workouts. Brands will appreciate the refined Finnish craftsmanship, while consumers get a punchy screen and lightweight design that feels great on daily runs.
Getting the Suunto Run 2 ready for action is a quick and smooth process using the updated companion app. The pairing process is fast, and the smartphone application serves as the main hub for customizing your sports profiles and data fields. Creating custom workouts is especially intuitive, allowing you to design structured interval sessions and push them straight to your wrist.
The standout software feature on the Suunto Run 2 is the addition of free offline topographic maps. Loading these maps onto your watch requires a quick setup step before heading out into the wild. You select your desired geographic region inside the Suunto app on your phone, place the watch on its charger, and connect to a wireless connection to start the download. Transferring map data over a wireless connection works smoothly, though larger regional map files can take a few hours to complete.
Operating the watch software is responsive and user-friendly, addressing the interface lag seen on older generation devices. Swiping up from the main watch face brings up your daily health widgets, while swiping right jumps straight into activity tracking. 
My main gripe with the Run 2 software is the same as the first series. For some reason, it operates a separate underlying software that is simply not as good as the options on every other watch in the Suunto stable. Notifications are clunky when received, and interactions are limited to only dismissing them from the watch and not the companion phone. It also doesn’t have access to the full 3rd-party apps and watch faces inside the Suunto app for further customizations.
Suunto has released very little on this, but it seems to be experimenting with a possible future OS variant with the cheaper Run series. Unfortunately, if you’ve used, or upgraded to, a more premium Suunto model, you get a frustratingly similar interface with just enough changes to drive you mad. Companies need to innovate for sure, but doing it on a device available to the public and doubling your existing consumer software stack just seems questionable.
With dual-band GPS, a stunning 2,000-nit AMOLED display, and full-color offline mapping, the Suunto Run 2 delivers premium hardware and accuracy that decisively undercuts Garmin on map value. However, jumping to a $350+ price tag pushes it into stiff competition from AmazFit/COROS and Suunto’s own Race S, leaving its stripped-down OS in an awkward middle ground.
In the performance department, the Suunto Run 2 packs high tier technology that delivers fantastic tracking accuracy. The watch features upgraded dual band multi satellite positioning that tracks your location across five satellite networks simultaneously. During outdoor testing, the GPS tracks remained remarkably accurate even when running past tall buildings or beneath thick tree canopy. Distance tracking stayed consistent and matched the performance of much more expensive flagship sports watches.
Heart rate tracking has received a major hardware upgrade thanks to a redesigned optical sensor array. The new sensor sits on a curved glass lens that rests closer against your wrist for better contact. During fast interval runs where optical wrist sensors usually lag behind, the Suunto Run 2 kept pace impressively well alongside a chest strap heart rate monitor. This accuracy makes it a fantastic tool for runners who base their training around heart rate zones.
The full color offline topographic maps look stunning on the two thousand nit AMOLED screen. Reading contour lines and trail paths on your wrist gives you excellent spatial awareness during off road adventures. While the maps do not show street names or provide automatic turn by turn rerouting, having full color terrain maps at this price point is a huge advantage over competitors.
Battery performance on the Suunto Run 2 receives a welcome boost thanks to a 29 percent larger battery capacity over the original. The watch packs a 388mAh battery inside its lightweight frame. In standard smartwatch mode with raise to wake enabled, you can easily expect over a week of daily battery life between charges. If you choose to leave the bright AMOLED display constantly on, battery life settles around four to five days.
Continuous tracking performance during workouts is equally solid for endurance athletes. Operating in the highest accuracy dual-band GPS mode gives you up to 24 hours of continuous tracking time. If you are taking on longer trail efforts or ultramarathons, you can easily adjust battery modes to extend tracking duration even further.
Suunto also updated the charging cable setup to match their flagship devices, using a unified magnetic charger that snaps securely onto the back of the watch case. It’s a small thing, but getting the Run 2 on the same charging hardware just simplifies SKUs and the lineup in general. Recharging the battery from empty to full takes about one to two hours. A quick top-up before heading out the door gives you plenty of juice for an extended outdoor training session.
Awarded to products with an average rating of 3.75 stars or higher, the AndroidGuys Smart Pick recognizes a balance of quality, performance, and value. 
Products with this distinction deserve to be on your short list of purchase candidates.
My struggle is where it fits into Suunto’s own offerings as well as the greater fitness watch market. My hold-up is that it runs a stripped-down alternative to even the rest of the Suunto lineup. With the new stainless-steel chassis, it’s essentially the same hardware as the more premium Suunto Race S and that watch is currently $20 cheaper. It also has some stiff competition from the likes of AmazFit Cheetah series and the COROS Pace at its new $350 price tag.
If you are looking for a comfortable running watch with gorgeous mapping and reliable sensors, the Suunto Run 2 is still a compelling choice. The Suunto Run 2 represents a hardware leap forward for Suunto in the entry sports watch category. By bringing dual band GPS, an ultra bright AMOLED display, and full-color offline mapping to a lightweight frame, Suunto has delivered a feature-rich tool for runners. While the price increase takes it out of the extreme budget tier, the performance upgrades mostly justify the dollar jump.

87,2026-09-17,"Australia will cut immigration by cracking down on international students and backpackers, Home Affairs Minister Tony Burke says 
International students, so-called ""visa hoppers"" and backpackers will be targeted under stricter rules announced by Australia on Thursday in a bid to curb immigration.
Foreign students will no longer be able to bring their families with them and those who want to extend their stay must choose a higher qualification.
Rules will also be stricter for working holiday visas - commonly used by backpackers and a crucial labour source for the agricultural sector - with a ballot system although these changes will not affect the UK.
The new rules were revealed by Home Affairs Minister Tony Burke who said that while immigration was a ""strength"" of Australia, there needed to be a ""high level of control"".
For international student and graduate visas, Burke said: ""We will no longer be adding to the stock of students automatically being able to bring family members over.""
There would be exemptions for foreign students already in Australia as well as applicants from the Pacific and South East Asian countries, he said.
PhD students would also be able to bring their families as they were in different life circumstances to undergraduates, Burke said. 
In a speech titled ""The work of managing the migration program: who arrives, who stays, who leaves"", Burke revealed a raft of changes as the government aims to bring net overseas migration to 225,000 people by 2028.
On student visas, he said the government would clamp down on ""visa hopping"" - where a student finishes a course, then applies to do another course which is often a lower qualification and sometimes with a ""not so legitimate provider"".
Students who want to go ""up the scale"" will be allowed, Burke said, for example, if a student finishes a bachelor's degree and then wants to complete a masters.
He also announced major changes to working holiday visas for those aged 18 to 30 years old who can work in Australia for 12 months, but can extend for a second and third year. 
Currently, these visa holders must work for three months in regional Australia during their first year to qualify for a second year, and then six months to qualify for a third year.
Under the new changes, those who qualify for a second year will have to go into a ballot with a cap of 45,000, down from the 57,000 who qualified last year.
For the third year, the ballot will be limited to 5,000, down from 31,000 who qualified last year. British working holidaymakers will not be affected by these changes due to the UK's free trade agreement with Australia.
New figures released by the Australian Bureau of Statistics on Thursday showed Australia's population grew 1.4% in the 12 months to March 2026 to 27.9m.
That total figure included births and deaths as well as a net overseas migration of 292,100. 
Migration remains a major political issue as debate around Australia's population grows amid rising living costs, housing affordability and increased strain on public services.
One Nation, a populist right-wing party known for its anti-immigration stance and which has seen a surge in support in recent months, detailed its migration policy earlier this week, proposing to cut temporary migrant visas by 750,000 over three years.
The party, which won its first lower house seat in May, also wants to cap net overseas immigration to 130,000, drawing a rebuke from Burke who said such a proposal would risk ""smashing parts of the Australian economy"".
In July, the government introduced a policy to prioritise applications for family visas  lodged onshore - where the applicant is in the country already - over those submitted overseas. 
Skilled migrants already in Australia who applied for permanent visas were also prioritised over those applying from overseas.
It also paused applications for working holiday visas from 24 countries, not including the UK.
Violet Roumeliotis, from Settlement Services International which helps new arrivals to Australia, says the migration debate was increasingly driven by ""short-term politics rather than what is best for the country"". 
""The proposed changes to family visas for international students is a good example of this,"" she said, adding that it would make it harder for Australia to compete for ""future talent"".

88,2026-09-17,"The SEC’s Innovation Exemption allows limited tokenized US stock trading on onchain venues with trading caps and transparency requirements.
The US Securities and Exchange Commission approved a temporary exemption allowing limited trading of tokenized US stocks on certain onchain venues.
Under the innovation exemption approved Thursday, Tokenized Securities Venues (TSVs) can offer permissioned trading of tokenized National Market System (NMS) stocks.
The exemption covers trading through automated market makers and liquidity pools, subject to requirements including transaction transparency, recordkeeping and technology safeguards, SEC Commissioner Mark Uyeda said. TSVs must also regularly publish US dollar-denominated transaction data, including prices, trade sizes, timestamps, pool addresses, end-of-day pool sizes and daily volumes.
“The Innovation Exemption is designed to be controlled,” Uyeda said, adding that symbol and volume limits will apply. He said the framework would give the SEC data to assess onchain securities trading and inform future rules.
The SEC is seeking public feedback on the framework, including data, case studies and information from live or test environments.
The SEC had been developing the innovation exemption for months. In February, SEC Chair Paul Atkins said the regulator was considering a temporary framework that would allow limited trading of tokenized securities through automated market makers while it developed longer-term rules.

90,2026-09-17,"From Nvidia and OpenAI to Anthropic and xAI, we break down the companies driving the AI boom and how much they are worth.
Save
Share
A renewed debate has emerged over who should control the development of artificial intelligence (AI).
The latest dispute follows an essay by Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei, who has argued that the development of increasingly powerful AI systems needs to slow down, calling for greater regulation.




US President Donald Trump pushed back, writing on Truth Social that the US already has “tremendous CRIMINAL and REGULATORY power” over AI companies, framing safety concerns as a conspiracy that only benefits China. He went on to say that whoever wins the AI race wins outright.
As states grapple with how to regulate an industry developing at an unprecedented pace, Al Jazeera unpacks the companies driving the AI race and the trillions of dollars behind them.
Chatbots and large language models are only the visible tip of the AI industry. Beneath them sits a much larger ecosystem of hardware, infrastructure, and software.
These companies can be broadly grouped into three categories:

At the foundation of the AI industry are the companies that design and manufacture the physical chips AI runs on, along with the equipment that keeps data centres powered and cooled.
These include:
These are the companies that turn raw chips into usable computing power, delivering the storage and processing capacity that AI systems run on.
These include:
These are the companies that create the AI products people and businesses actually use.
These include:
The 10 largest public companies operating in the AI space – spanning AI chips, cloud infrastructure, and AI-driven software and services – have a combined market capitalisation of at least $25 trillion.
That’s bigger than the gross domestic product (GDP) of every country except the United States, and larger than the entire Chinese economy.
The graphic below shows the 20 most valuable public companies with direct exposure to AI, ranked by market capitalisation.
Nvidia is the largest AI-focused public company, with a market capitalisation of roughly $5.1 trillion as of mid-September 2026. It designs graphics processing units (GPUs) and AI accelerators – hardware components in a chip that speed up AI workloads – that power leading AI models. It makes its money by selling these chips to hyperscalers and AI companies.
Apple, while primarily a hardware company, has invested heavily in on-device AI features and its market cap of $4.86 trillion makes it one of the most valuable companies with any AI exposure. Its main AI product, Siri AI, launched in 2026 and runs on Google’s Gemini models under a licensing deal.
Taiwan Semiconductor (TSMC) is the world’s largest chip manufacturer, with a market cap of $1.9 trillion. It makes the most advanced chips, designed by the likes of Nvidia, Broadcom, and AMD. It makes its money by manufacturing these chips at enormous scale, and currently controls over 70 percent of the global foundry market.
Additionally, several private AI companies are valued in the billions of dollars, with some expected to go public in the coming months.
Anthropic, valued at about $965bn (May 2026), develops the Claude family of AI models. The company filed for an initial public offering (IPO) in June 2026 to sell stocks publicly, and is reportedly targeting a listing as early as October 2026.
OpenAI, valued at about $852bn, develops the GPT models behind ChatGPT. OpenAI filed for an IPO in June 2026, but is now reportedly leaning toward a 2027 listing.
xAI, now merged into SpaceX, develops the Grok family of AI models. At the time of the February 2026 merger, xAI alone was valued at about $250bn.
